## Glossary

Air purification
Removal of contaminants from the air, including allergens, odours and
pathogens, to improve the quality of indoor air, and which may be used
to prevent the transmission of certain respiratory infections (1). Ways in
which this is achieved include air-purifying devices that pull air through
either a filter to trap contaminants, ionizers that make particles dense
and fall to the ground/surface and ultraviolet light devices that inactivate
pathogens that transmit through the air. Promising technologies for
the removal of pathogens in particular are high efficiency particulate
air (HEPA) filtration with the ability to remove > 99% of aerosols (2) and
germicidal ultraviolet light (GUVL). Air purification reuses the same air
while ventilation pushes out indoor air and replaces it with outdoor air.
Active case-finding
Measures and actions implemented to identify, track and manage
and contact
cases and exposed individuals in the community (for example, contact
identification
tracing, testing and quarantine) to reduce transmission of an infectious
measures
pathogen.
Alcohol-based
An alcohol-containing preparation (liquid, gel or foam) designed
handrub
for application to the hands to inactivate microorganisms and/or
temporarily suppress their growth. Such preparations may contain
one or more types of alcohol, other active ingredients with excipients
and humectants (3, 4).
Close contact
A person who has been in close proximity, including touching, speaking
with, providing care or being less than 1 metre away from another
person.
Contact tracing
The identification and follow-up of persons who may have come into
contact with an infected and contagious person and who may therefore
be at risk of becoming infected (5).
Entry and exit
The preliminary assessment of a traveller who is entering or exiting a
screening at points
country by an authorized health worker or by a person under the direct
of entry
supervision of the competent authority, to determine the person’s health
status and potential public health risk to others, and which may include
the scrutinizing of health documents and a physical examination when
justified by the circumstances of the individual case. In this guideline,
entry and exit screening at points of entry specifically refers to syndromic
screening for influenza using one or more methods, such as temperature
check, observation and health declaration forms.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 10 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 9 -->

ix
Environmental
Measures taken to limit the transmission of a respiratory pathogen with
measures
epidemic or pandemic potential through decontamination or other
modification of the environment.
Hand hygiene
Any action of hand cleansing such as handwashing, antiseptic
handwashing, antiseptic handrubbing or surgical hand antisepsis (3, 4).
Handwashing
Washing hands with plain or antimicrobial soap and water.
Human rights
The meaning and scope of human rights as provided for in international
human rights treaties and as interpreted and applied by the human
rights bodies tasked with this work, which can include international,
regional and national courts, and human rights committees.
Humidifier
A device for increasing the moisture level in the air within a room or other
enclosed space.
Indoor hospitality and
Measures taken by the indoor catering, leisure, fitness and personal care
recreation measures
service industries to reduce the transmission of a respiratory pathogen.
Isolation of
Separation or restriction of movement of ill persons with an infectious
symptomatic and/or
disease to prevent transmission to others (6). This can involve the isolation
infected individuals
of ill persons with an infectious disease in a health-care facility (7) or
isolation or confinement at home (often not needing hospitalization)
(6, 7). Voluntary isolation (also known as self-isolation) is the voluntary
separation or restriction of movement of ill persons in a designated
space, usually in their own homes, but possibly be elsewhere (6).
Lateral flow test
A rapid diagnostic test suitable for point-of-care testing that identifies
infections by detecting a viral target substance (antigens or antibodies)
in a liquid sample (for example, urine, blood or saliva). When such
target substances are present in the sample, they bind to the reactive
molecules and show a visual positive result based on the principle of
affinity chromatography. Rapid antigen tests (RATs) are a type of lateral
flow test.
Mask wearing
The wearing of a mask (such as a medical mask) to block large
respiratory particles, splashes, sprays or splatter that may contain
infectious agents from coming into contact with the face of the
wearer (8). Masks may also help to reduce the exposure of others to the
wearer’s saliva and respiratory secretions (8). In the current document,
mask wearing is considered only in the community context.
Mass testing
Testing for a specific pathogen in an entire population or subpopulation
regardless of symptoms to identify cases during a specific timeframe or
at regular intervals.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 11 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 10 -->

x
Medical mask
A personal protective equipment device, flat or pleated, that covers the
(also known as surgical
nose and mouth, and it is affixed to the head of the user with straps
or procedure mask)
around the ears, head or both. Its performance is tested according
to a set of standardized methods that aim to balance high filtration,
adequate breathability and, optionally, fluid penetration resistance (9).
Mitigation
The action of reducing the transmission of an infectious pathogen in
order to decrease its mortality and morbidity burden and its impact on
the health system.
Modifying indoor
Altering the amount of water vapour in the air in indoor environments.
humidity
For example, humidifiers are devices used to increase the moisture level
in the air in a room or other enclosed space.
Non-pharmaceutical
See Public health and social measures below.
interventions
Personal protection
Measures taken by individuals to reduce their personal risk of acquiring
measures
or spreading an infectious disease; this includes the use of personal
protective equipment.
Physical distancing
Maintaining a specific physical distance between people in public areas,
for example by limiting the capacity of indoor or outdoor venues where
gatherings occur.
Point of entry
A passage for the international entry or exit of travellers, baggage,
cargo, containers, conveyances, goods and postal parcels, as well as
agencies and areas providing services to them on entry or exit (10).
Public health and
Non-pharmaceutical interventions implemented by individuals,
social measures
communities, institutions and governments at community, national,
regional and global levels to protect the health and well-being of
populations affected by health emergencies. PHSM aim to reduce
the risk and scale of transmission of infectious diseases by reducing
transmission-relevant exposures and/or making them safer (11).
Quarantine of exposed
Restriction of activities and/or separation from others of individuals who
individuals
are not ill but have had close contact with a proven or suspected case of
an infectious disease, in such a manner as to prevent the possible spread
of infection (10). Such sequestration of individuals lasts for a defined
period after their last exposure.
Rapid antigen test
A type of lateral flow test used for point-of-care testing that directly
detects the presence of an antigen. It can be used to detect influenza
infections in the community (separately from any clinical testing in
ambulatory settings or hospitals). Such point-of-care tests may be
administered to the entire population (see Mass testing above), to
subpopulations (including those attending schools) or to facilitate
self-testing at home.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 12 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 11 -->

xi
Respiratory hygiene
A personal protection measure to reduce the transmission of a
and cough etiquette
respiratory pathogen by covering the nose and mouth with a bent
elbow or disposable tissue while coughing or sneezing, followed by the
immediate and hygienic disposal of used tissues and the performing of
hand hygiene (12).
Restrictions on
The restriction of social gatherings such as religious pilgrimages, major
gatherings
sporting and cultural events, festivals, weddings or funerals.
R0
Basic reproduction number; a measure of transmissibility. This number
is generally estimated at the beginning of an epidemic or pandemic,
and represents the average number of people infected by one infectious
case in a completely susceptible population.
Rt
Effective reproduction number; a measure of transmissibility. This
number represents the current average number of people infected by
one infectious case in a population at any given time. After the initial
spread of an infection in a community, the value of Rt may fluctuate over
time due to public health and social measures, decreases in susceptible
individuals or increases in population immunity.
School closure
Closure of a school or schools to all children and staff. Reactive school
closure occurs after substantial transmission of the respiratory pathogen
has been observed in the school or community, while proactive school
closure occurs before substantial transmission among children and staff
has been observed.
School measures
Adaptive strategies implemented to reduce the transmission of a
respiratory virus in schools, or on the way to and from schools, by
decreasing the frequency and intensity of interpersonal interactions.
Social measures
Measures that apply to all members or certain segments of the
community regardless of their infection/exposure status, and typically
aimed at reducing the frequency and intensity of interpersonal
interactions.
Stay-at-home
Public recommendations or decrees to prevent people from leaving
provisions
their homes for anything other than essential activities (such as grocery
shopping or limited exercise).
Surface and object
Routine cleaning of frequently used surfaces and objects to reduce
cleaning and
transmission of infectious pathogens. Disinfection may then be carried
disinfection
out using ultraviolet light or products such as chlorine, bleach or alcohol.
Cleaning should always precede disinfection to remove dirt and
impurities from surfaces and objects.

models of employment.
Health information and advice on potential health risks provided by
health authorities to help travellers understand how best to avoid or
minimize these risks, take appropriate preventive measures and exercise
necessary precautions before, during and after travel. In the context
of this guideline, travel advice specifically refers to health advice or
warnings or recommendations which aim to prevent or limit influenza
Public health measures concerning travellers, baggage, cargo,
containers, conveyances and goods (for example, the provision of travel
health advice, screening of travellers and travel restrictions).
The restriction of movement of persons, baggage, cargo, containers,
conveyances and goods, for example through travel bans, border
closures, flight suspensions, banning ground crossings, restricting ship
itineraries, and refusal of entry through visa suspensions or denial.
The sequestration, for a defined period upon arrival, of individuals,
regardless of exposure or infection status, travelling from a high-risk
area, with the aim of reducing the likelihood of importing cases, thus
delaying the onset of an epidemic in the community.
The process of supplying outdoor air (or outdoor air plus recirculated
air that has been treated) and removing indoor air from a space,
for the purpose of controlling air contaminant levels, by natural or
mechanical means (13). Natural ventilation involves the exchange of air
in a building through windows, doors, solar chimneys, wind towers or
trickle ventilators, while mechanical ventilation uses mechanical fans to
drive the outdoor air into an indoor space at a designed airflow rate.
Treatment of the outdoor air may be needed before introducing it into
the building if local outdoor air conditions require it (for example, due to
high particulate matter concentration). The basic elements of building
ventilation are the ventilation rate, airflow direction and air distribution
Reactive closure of offices and businesses when the transmission of
the respiratory pathogen has been observed in the workplace, or the
proactive closure of all workplaces irrespective of the infection status
Adaptive strategies implemented to reduce respiratory virus
transmission in workplaces, or on the way to and from work, by
decreasing the frequency and intensity of interpersonal interactions, and
including policies to encourage working from home or hybrid working

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 15 -->

xiv
to be made, the GDG used the established evidence-to-decision framework approach. Where
a strong recommendation was made, the GDG was confident that the desirable effects of the
intervention outweighed any undesirable effects. Where there was uncertainty concerning
the balance between desirable and undesirable effects, the GDG issued a conditional
recommendation. Unanimous agreement was reached on all of the recommendations contained
in this guideline. All potential conflicts of interest for the individual experts constituting the GDG
were declared, assessed and managed in line with WHO procedures.
A total of 21 recommendations in five areas (sections 4–8 below) were developed and are
summarized in Table 1 of the current document. When making decisions on the selection and
implementation of interventions, countries must comply with domestic and international laws
and obligations, including human rights laws and the obligations set out in IHR (2005). In this
regard, consideration should be given to the balance of benefits and harms, the feasibility and
acceptability of proposed interventions in the local context, anticipated barriers to effective
implementation, and the potential impacts of measure on vulnerable and marginalized groups.
Consideration should also be given to how best to maintain access to health-care services for
other health conditions, and to the medium and long-term repercussions of interventions on
society and the wider economy.
In general, limiting the number and intensity of interactions between infected and uninfected
people through both targeted and community-wide measures is an effective strategy for
reducing respiratory virus transmission in the community. Such a strategy is likely to significantly
reduce the total number of infections, hospitalizations and deaths, thereby also reducing the
peak demand for hospital beds. However, both targeted and community-wide measures
can be highly disruptive, and their potential beneficial impact on community transmission of
an epidemic or pandemic influenza virus must be weighed against the socioeconomic and
other costs of such measures. Early risk assessments of the potential impact of an epidemic
or pandemic influenza virus will help public health authorities determine the stringency of
interventions required to mitigate its impact. For all influenza epidemics and pandemics,
recommending the isolation of symptomatic and/or infected individuals at home should help to
reduce transmission, and policies that facilitate the voluntary isolation of such individuals (such as
hybrid working models and paid sick leave) should be prioritized. For more severe pandemics,
measures to increase physical distancing in schools, workplaces and public areas could further
reduce transmission. In all cases, consideration should also be given to putting in place measures
aimed at minimizing the socioeconomic impact of disruptive measures on affected individuals.
This document is intended to serve as a core component of WHO’s influenza prevention
and control programme in community settings. The successful implementation of the
recommendations provided will depend upon the systematic integration of PHSM into health
emergency prevention, preparedness and response plans at national and subnational
levels, and on the appropriate application of its recommendations alongside other PHSM
implementation guidance.

The three major influenza pandemics of the 20th century were the 1918 “Spanish flu”, the 1957
“Asian flu” and the 1968 “Hong Kong flu” (Table 3). The most severe of these was the 1918
pandemic caused by the A(H1N1) virus, which resulted in 20–50 million deaths and had a
particularly notable impact on mortality in young adults (31). The 1957 and 1968 pandemics
(caused by A(H2N2) and A(H3N2) viruses respectively) each caused around 1 million deaths
worldwide, with the greatest impact being on mortality in older adults (32).
The first influenza pandemic of the 21st century occurred in 2009 and was caused by a new strain
of the influenza A(H1N1) virus that was antigenically distinct from the seasonal A(H1N1) strains
circulating at the same time but antigenically similar to A(H1N1) strains that had circulated prior
to 1950 (33). The virus is thought to have emerged in Central America shortly before it was first
detected in North America in April 2009 and subsequently spread rapidly to other parts of the
world (34) Because of the similarity with older A(H1N1) viruses, older adults had some immunity

pandemic was estimated to have caused 123 000–203 000 respiratory deaths (36).

Influenza pandemics typically occur in epidemic waves. For example, in 2009, the United States
of America (USA) experienced a spring epidemic of A(H1N1)pdm09 that had only a limited
impact. However, this was followed by a much larger autumn epidemic that had a major
health impact (38).

### The origin of pandemics

A much greater range of influenza A virus subtypes circulates in animals, particularly in wild
aquatic birds. Although human infections with avian influenza A subtypes are sporadic, there is
a risk that these strains will develop the capacity for effective human-to-human transmission,
resulting in the next pandemic. Of all the avian influenza A subtypes, the emergence of highly
pathogenic A(H5N1) in 1997 has raised most concern because of the severity of laboratoryconfirmed human infections (39). In addition, more than 1000 laboratory-confirmed human
infections with the avian influenza A(H7N9) virus occurred in China in the period 2013–2018 (40) –
though with no indication of sustained human-to-human transmission (41). Several other avian
influenza A subtypes, including A(H9N2), A(H6N1) and A(H7N7), have also caused sporadic
human infections (42). As demonstrated in 2009, influenza pandemics can also emerge
from swine influenza viruses. In all cases, only influenza A viruses are known to have caused
pandemics (43).
1.1.2
Human influenza virus transmission

### Contact transmission

Contact transmission can be either direct or indirect. Transmission via direct physical contact
(for example, kissing) can occur between an infected individual and a susceptible individual.
Transmission via indirect contact occurs through an intermediate object (for example, when a
susceptible individual touches a contaminated surface or object and then touches their mouth,
nose or eyes without performing hand hygiene) (18). Several studies have shown that influenza
viruses can survive for prolonged periods on certain types of surfaces and can survive on hands
for a short time (44).

[TABLA COMPLEJA - CONSULTAR PDF ORIGINAL, PAGINA 22]

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 23 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 22 -->

4
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Airborne transmission/inhalation

Influenza viruses can be detected in the fine-particle aerosols emitted into the air by infected
individuals during exhalation, coughing or sneezing (45). These infectious respiratory particles
can then enter the respiratory tract of another person through inhalation, potentially causing
infection (18, 46). Although this form of transmission is likely to occur at close range because of
virus dilution and inactivation over distance and time, these particles can remain suspended in
the air for extended periods and may be responsible for higher rates of transmission, particularly
in crowded areas (47).

### Direct deposition

Direct deposition (previously known as “droplet transmission”) is the spread of an infectious
agent by dissemination of infectious respiratory particles. After emission from an infected
person, these particles follow a semi-ballistic trajectory and then deposit on the exposed facial
mucosa surfaces (mouth, nose and eyes) of another person (46, 48). Virus-laden particles are
expelled into the environment by talking, breathing, coughing or sneezing, usually travel short
distances (1–2 metres from the source) and do not remain suspended in the air (47, 49). Although
direct deposition is a potential route of influenza transmission, there is limited evidence to
support this (50).

### Impact of modes of transmission on the effectiveness of PHSM

The various modes of transmission each have implications for the effectiveness of measures
against influenza transmission. However, uncertainty over the specific contributions of contact
and through the air transmission has hindered the optimization of control strategies. In settings
where influenza virus transmission occurs through multiple modes, implementing measures to
mitigate one mode of transmission (for example, through frequent hand hygiene) may not be
sufficient to reduce overall transmission (51). Isolating infected individuals – that is, keeping them
away from others – is likely to reduce transmission by all modes.
1.1.3
Public health and social measures (PHSM)
PHSM refer to non-pharmaceutical interventions implemented by individuals, communities
and governments during health emergencies to reduce the risk and scale of transmission of an
infectious disease. PHSM decrease the risk and scale of infectious disease outbreaks by reducing
exposure to the pathogen or making exposure situations safer. The mode of transmission of
a disease (for example, through the air, direct/indirect contact, waterborne or foodborne) will
determine the selection of interventions and their combination. PHSM are often the first and
sometimes the only interventions available in the early stages of a pandemic, when effective
vaccines and therapeutics are not yet available or equitably accessible (52). PHSM also play
a critical role throughout the different stages of health emergencies and act in concert with
medical countermeasures, when available.
In order to protect individuals and communities during the first and then subsequent waves of an
epidemic or pandemic, PHSM aim:
1.
To delay the start of an epidemic or pandemic in unaffected countries – this is particularly
important if the resulting delay is long enough to allow for effective vaccines to be
produced and distributed to reduce the impact of the outbreak.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 24 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 23 -->

5
| 1. Introduction
2.
To delay the peak of the epidemic or pandemic – once an epidemic has started, PHSM may
be used to delay the peak of the epidemic, again allowing time for vaccines to be produced
and distributed, and/or for the health-care system to better prepare for a surge in cases.
3.
To reduce the peak of an epidemic or pandemic – by reducing transmission in the
community, the outbreak may be spread out over a longer period, with a reduced epidemic
or pandemic peak. This can be particularly important if the health-care system has limited
resources or capacity (for example, in terms of hospital beds and ventilators). When healthcare systems maintain their capacity to manage and treat symptomatic and severe cases,
overall morbidity and mortality can be reduced even if the total number of infections across
the epidemic or pandemic is not reduced.
4.
To reduce the total number of infections – some interventions (such as physical distancing
measures) may aim to reduce the total number of infections, which in turn will also reduce
the total number of severe cases, hospitalizations and deaths.
A graphical summary of the above aims and intended impacts of PHSM during an influenza
epidemic or pandemic is presented in Fig. 1.

Each of these objectives should contribute towards reducing the overall impact of the epidemic
or pandemic. PHSM used in community settings could focus on reducing transmission-relevant
contacts or making them safer through personal protection measures (such as hand hygiene and
mask wearing), reducing disease spread in the community (for example, by closing schools and
placing restrictions on gatherings) and/or limiting international spread (for example, through the
imposition of travel restrictions) (5).

[TABLA COMPLEJA - CONSULTAR PDF ORIGINAL, PAGINA 24]

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 25 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 24 -->

6
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
Determining the suitability of individual PHSM will depend upon the objectives (and specific
transmission routes to be acted on), as well as contextual factors, and equity and human rights
considerations. To avoid exacerbating existing health and social inequities as an unintended
consequence of PHSM, health, equity and social justice considerations should be placed at the
core of decision-making and implementation. A fair and equitable response will require that
PHSM are designed, implemented, adjusted and lifted with the needs of communities, and in
particular vulnerable and marginalized populations, in mind. In addition, there will be a need
to ensure the involvement of all affected communities in the decision-making process. Social
protection policies and programmes such as paid sick leave or unemployment benefits should
be implemented alongside PHSM to reduce negative consequences such as loss of income,
food insecurity, gender and social inequity, and slowed economic productivity. Other mitigation
measures led by communities, such as food delivery services for groups at high risk or child care
initiatives, can further alleviate the socioeconomic burdens of implementing PHSM.
1.1.4
PHSM for the management and control of influenza epidemics
and pandemics
With no vaccine available during the 1918 A(H1N1) pandemic, its management relied on the
implementation of PHSM. Measures included isolation, quarantine and restrictions on gatherings
and were implemented on a large-scale and likely saved lives (54, 55). In contrast, PHSM were
only used in the first few months of the 2009 A(H1N1) pandemic due to its lower health impact
compared to the three influenza pandemics of the 20th century (56).
The limited evidence available on the relative importance of each of the potential modes of
influenza transmission limits the strength of recommendations for some PHSM aimed at blocking
specific transmission routes. For example, enhancing hand hygiene will reduce indirect contact
transmission but it is unclear whether indirect contact transmission is a major contributor to
transmission overall in the community. The literature suggests that influenza virus transmission
mostly occurs indoors at quite close range through the respiratory route, involving infectious
respiratory particles of various sizes including very small particles that can remain suspended in
the air for a prolonged period. If this is the case, measures to reduce transmission could aim to
increase the physical distance between individuals, reduce the number of individuals in the same
location at the same time and improve indoor ventilation.
The effectiveness of PHSM is also influenced by the transmission dynamics of the epidemic
or pandemic influenza virus strain. As a significant proportion of influenza virus infections are
asymptomatic (57), the isolation of sick and/or symptomatic patients may only capture a fraction
of the total cases. Different influenza subtypes and lineages also have different attack rates,
transmission potential and severity profiles in different age groups. For example, children tend to
shed more influenza viruses in general and do so for longer than adults throughout the course of
an infection (58). Influenza B virus infections also tend to be more severe in children than adults.
Previous studies have suggested that seasonal influenza epidemics could be mitigated by timelimited school closures (59, 60). Therefore, from an epidemiological perspective, the selection
of PHSM to control influenza epidemics and pandemics needs to take into consideration the
transmission dynamics of the specific strain.

PHSM for the management and control of the COVID-19 pandemic
The emergence of the severe acute respiratory coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) in late 2019 (61)
and over 7 million deaths having occurred as of 18 August 2024 (62). The rapid emergence
of this novel coronavirus meant that no vaccines or effective antiviral treatments to mitigate
its public health impact were available during the first year of the pandemic, with access
to such medical countermeasures remaining inequitable even after their development and
deployment. Consequently, PHSM played a key role in the pandemic response – with the

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 27 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 26 -->

8
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
During the first year of the COVID-19 pandemic, the use of PHSM to reduce SARS-CoV-2
transmission was associated with a concurrent significant reduction in influenza activity globally,
with such activity becoming minimal during the second year when the implementation of PHSM
was most stringent and widespread.1 Furthermore, after PHSM were gradually rolled back
in the third year of the COVID-19 pandemic, influenza activity levels increased, suggesting a
potential causal relationship between the implementation of stringent PHSM and the reduction
of influenza transmission in the community. While there are important differences between the
transmission dynamics of SARS-CoV-2 and influenza, the near-complete suppression of influenza
activity during the COVID-19 pandemic is instructive and has informed the systematic evaluation
of the scientific literature that forms the basis of the current document.
The widespread use of PHSM during the COVID-19 pandemic also highlighted a number
of contextual factors that significantly impacted the effectiveness of individual measures.
For example, the capacity of the health-care system to isolate and effectively treat infected
individuals differed significantly between and within countries. In addition, cultural norms and
the uptake of, and ability to adhere to, PHSM varied significantly across different communities –
for example, individuals in precarious working conditions who were dependent on daily wages
for onsite work were unable to adhere to isolation recommendations in the absence of paid sick
leave or protection from job loss.
Most importantly, the COVID-19 pandemic highlighted the ways in which the effectiveness of
PHSM can be significantly influenced by social inequity, and the ways in which such measures
can exacerbate existing inequalities in communities (66). This underlines the importance of
incorporating equity and human rights considerations into national pandemic preparedness
planning to ensure the effective implementation of PHSM that are both necessary and
proportionate to the health impact of the epidemic or pandemic, while respecting international
human rights obligations (67).
1.1.6
History of WHO guidance on the use of PHSM during influenza
pandemics
WHO guidance on national and regional influenza pandemic preparedness planning, and on the
role of WHO in pandemic preparedness and response activities, was first developed in 1999 (68).
In 2005, this document was updated to include a pandemic preparedness “checklist” to further
guide and refine national planning efforts (69). The checklist included the need to plan for the
use of public health measures to prevent the spread of a pandemic pathogen in the community
based on the following rationale:
As the access to vaccines and antiviral drugs during a pandemic will be extremely
limited, especially in countries with limited resources, nonmedical interventions
may be the only way to delay the spread of the disease. However, many of these
interventions may affect human behaviour and human rights, and therefore need a
strong educational, legal and well-supported basis. Moreover, most of the interventions
are based on limited evidence. Therefore, transparent decision-making and frank
information-sharing should go hand in hand with the measures discussed in this section.

#### 1.2.2 Target audience

This guideline is intended for use by national and local health authorities, and stakeholders
from other sectors, including the educational sector, involved in the decision-making or
implementation of PHSM during an influenza epidemic or pandemic. It is intended that users
can use the guideline to determine the most appropriate measures to use, and when, during
an influenza outbreak. It could also serve as a supporting resource for the development and
updating of national preparedness plans for mitigating the adverse consequences and impacts
of influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### 1.3 International Health Regulations (2005)2

The scope and purpose of the International Health Regulations (2005) (IHR) (10) are to prevent,
protect against, control and provide a public health response to, the international spread of
disease in ways that are commensurate with and restricted to public health risks, and which
avoid unnecessary interference with international traffic and trade (Article 2). Respecting human
rights is a cross-cutting principle of the IHR, which requires that “the implementation of these
Regulations shall be with full respect for the dignity, human rights and fundamental freedoms
of persons” (Article 3). The IHR aims to protect health for the common good of the international
community, taking into account the sovereignty of individual States Parties.
Under the IHR, States Parties are entitled to implement additional public health measures to
protect the health of their populations during public health events, provided that such measures
are in accordance with their relevant national laws and obligations under international law, are
based on scientific evidence, and are neither more restrictive of international trade nor more
onerous or intrusive than reasonably available alternatives.
A key obligation of the Regulations for States Parties is that they must develop, strengthen and
maintain core public health capacities for surveillance and response. In order to be able to
detect, assess, notify and report events and respond to public health risks and emergencies of
international concern. States Parties must meet the requirements described in the IHR, including,
at the national level, the assessment of all reports of urgent events within 48 hours and the
immediate reporting to WHO through the National IHR Focal Point, when required.
In relation to influenza specifically, Annex 1 of the IHR requires States Parties to notify WHO
within 24 hours of any laboratory-confirmed case of human influenza caused by a new subtype
according to the WHO case definition (76). Human influenza infections caused by a new subtype
has been established as being unusual or unexpected, and may have serious public health
impacts. For this reason, even a single confirmed case of such an infection that fulfils the WHO
case definition must always be notified immediately to WHO, regardless of the context in which
2

WHA77.17 which were notified to States Parties on 19 September and will normally come into force on 19
September 2025 for 192 States Parties. Revision of the current guideline may be considered, if appropriate,
following the entry into force of the said amendments.

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
it occurs. For events involving suspected cases of human influenza caused by a new subtype (for
example, in the absence of laboratory confirmation), States Parties are required to carry out an
assessment according to the decision instrument provided in the IHR, and to then notify WHO of
all qualifying events within 24 hours of such an assessment. Notifications and other event-related
communications under the IHR are carried out (using the most efficient means of communication
available) between the National IHR Focal Point on behalf of the State Party concerned and the
WHO IHR Contact Point at the respective WHO regional office.

### 1.4 Pandemic influenza severity assessment


## framework

The pandemic influenza severity assessment (PISA) framework was introduced by WHO in 2017
and updated in 2024 (77). The severity of an influenza epidemic or pandemic is evaluated and
monitored through four indicators: transmissibility (referring to incidence), seriousness of disease,
morbidity and mortality, and impact on health-care capacity. Severity for each indicator is
categorized into one of five levels – no activity or below seasonal threshold, low, moderate,
high or extraordinary in comparison to historical levels. The PISA approach is intended to be
used routinely, during both seasonal influenza epidemics and pandemics as a visualization
and communication tool to help public health authorities monitor and assess the severity of an
influenza outbreak, communicate risk and inform appropriate decisions and recommendations
on interventions. Of particular relevance to the current guideline on the use of PHSM, the PISA
evaluation of severity may inform the selection and timing of interventions (for example, by
providing a rationale for the use of highly disruptive measures).

### 1.5 Equity and human rights

Human rights are recognized and protected in the WHO Constitution, the IHR and national laws.
In addition, human rights are enshrined in a range of international human rights instruments,
including international treaties that are binding on countries and overseen by treaty monitoring
bodies (Annex 1).
In the context of a health emergency like a pandemic, public health and human rights are
intrinsically linked. As PHSM may require restrictions on individual rights, they must be necessary
(following proper scientific evidence), proportionate to the public health threat and time limited,
and non-arbitrary (non-discriminatory) (67). PHSM are therefore both required by the right
to health and strengthened when implemented in accordance with human rights principles.
Moreover, taking into account the impact of PHSM on economic and social rights, and the
connection between health and the social determinants of health, their implementation must
accord with the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights, including the
rights to education, housing, water, an adequate standard of living and social security (78). In
these and other respects, PHSM should be grounded in international and domestic human rights
laws, and be designed to protect the rights of people to health and life, including by avoiding or
minimizing interference with rights such as education, work, protection from violence, housing,
food, freedom of movement, assembly and peaceful assembly and privacy.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 32 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 31 -->

13
| 1. Introduction
To prevent inequity including gender inequality, discrimination and stigma arising from the use
of PHSM, they should be designed and implemented with full attention to the human rights of all
vulnerable and disadvantaged groups (79, 80). Measures should neither directly nor indirectly
(that is, explicitly or inadvertently) discriminate against such groups. Moreover, a human
rights-based approach includes the participation of vulnerable and marginalized communities
in the design, implementation and oversight of PHSM to ensure equitable and effective
policies (81). In some cases, positive affirmative measures, mitigations and adjustments may be
needed to reduce the disproportionate risks faced by some groups during a severe influenza
outbreak. Mitigation measures may include social protection policies and programmes and
community-led initiatives to reduce and compensate for any interference by PHSM on human
rights including those integrally linked to livelihoods and well-being (82). Building capacity and
resources for the right to health care, including access to treatment and vaccines, can also
reduce the risk of interference with human rights associated with some PHSM (83).
The United Nations Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights General Comment No 14
(2000), the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (1966) and the Siracusa Principles
on the Limitation and Derogation Provisions in the International Covenant on Civil and Political
Rights (1984) (84) recognize that certain human rights may be restricted to protect public health
in emergencies, providing any such measures are:
•
in accordance with the law;
•
pursue a legitimate aim, that is, to protect public health;
necessary and proportionate in relation to that objective (that is, only implemented after a
comprehensive examination of less restrictive alternatives and where a failure to act would
be even more detrimental to human rights);
•
not arbitrary;
•
non-discriminatory; and
•
subject to review and lifted in the minimum time needed.
The Siracusa Principles further provide that “public health may be invoked as a ground for
limiting certain rights in order to allow a state to take measures dealing with a serious threat
to the health of the population or individual members of the population. These measures must
be specifically aimed at preventing disease or injury or providing care for the sick and injured”
and that “due regard shall be had to the international health regulations of the World Health
Organization”.
The implementation of PHSM should also be conducted according to principles of least restrictive
means, legality, non-discrimination, necessity and proportionality. For example, the use of lawful
enforcement should be a last resort and only where strictly necessary for pressing public health
purposes and where less restrictive means are ineffective. The use of criminal sanctions should
be avoided to the extent possible (85), with any such sanctions subject to review and set at a
level that does not lead to hardship. In fact, compulsory restrictive and punitive measures tend
to exacerbate barriers for the people most in need and potentially increase their vulnerabilities
(86). Instead, building trust and empowering communities with the information they need to both
protect themselves and help others are key to an effective response during emergencies.
Where a country implements measures that result in a derogation to human rights based on a
state of emergency or similar state of exception, any such measures must meet a specific threat
to the life of the nation, be pursuant to a publicly proclaimed state of emergency and must not

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 33 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 32 -->

14
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
interfere with non-derogable rights.3 Such measures should also be temporary, legal, necessary,
proportionate and non-discriminatory (84, 87, 88).
Regular review and, where necessary, revision of policies on PHSM implementation should
be undertaken to ensure adherence with human rights principles as set out in international
treaties. Such processes should be deliberative and involve public participation, including by
marginalized and disadvantaged groups. In addition, ensuring access to prompt and effective
judicial and/or equivalent administrative remedies is an important guarantee of rights for
individuals and populations, including during public health emergencies (85).

### 1.6 Risk communication and community


## engagement

Ensuring access to public health information is critical during a pandemic to protect the
right to health. However, the dissemination of information alone is not sufficient to ensure
that populations at risk of disease will follow public health advice. Those affected by health
emergencies should be consulted as important partners in the development of PHSM, and
this should be a critical part of all risk communication and community engagement (RCCE)
strategies (89).
RCCE is a technical discipline, involving two interlinked concepts:
•
Risk communication involves the real-time exchange of information, advice and opinions
between experts or officials and those members of the public who are facing health
hazards. It enables people to make informed decisions to mitigate the effects of a threat
and to take protective and preventive measures, and is particularly important during an
emergency such as a pandemic.
•
Community engagement brings people into the process by helping them to understand
the risks and contribute to the development of acceptable, workable health and response
practices. By engaging in two-way communication, public health authorities can gather
feedback, address concerns and better tailor their communications to the target audience.
Managing information during a disease outbreak is also crucial. Infodemic management –
that is, handling the flood of both accurate and false information in the physical and digital
space (90) – is a critical part of RCCE. It aims to create healthy and trustworthy information
environments by identifying and addressing concerns, information voids, misinformation and
disinformation, and by responding to community concerns.
1.6.1
Promoting acceptance and uptake of PHSM
Effective RCCE, along with infodemic management, is critical in promoting healthy behaviours,
preventing the spread of disease and managing public health crises, and is pivotal in promoting
3
Non-derogable rights are absolute rights that cannot be suspended or infringed upon under any circumstances
and include the right to life, freedom from torture and cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment, and freedom
from slavery or servitude.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 34 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 33 -->

15
| 1. Introduction
the acceptance and uptake of PHSM (91). It helps to build and maintain trust between
communities and health authorities, raise awareness of influenza risks and transmission modes,
and explain associated PHSM. Reducing confusion and promoting accurate knowledge through
effective RCCE also fosters collaboration between health authorities and communities, thus
contributing significantly to the overall effectiveness of PHSM (92).
There are numerous theoretical models that can help define “effective” RCCE approaches (92),
all which overlap with regard to the need to establish trust with at-risk communities by being
transparent, honest and reliable, while ensuring the clarity and consistency of messages
(92, 93). The WHO RCCE competency framework provides detailed information on the specific
competencies required by RCCE teams to fulfil these actions (94).
The remainder of this section summarizes the key components of RCCE readiness and
response actions.

### Maintain engagement with communities even in the absence of an emergency

Readiness, in the context of disease outbreaks, is a country’s ability to respond to a health
threat quickly and appropriately when required. Readiness activities are a foundation for
response efforts when outbreaks occur, and RCCE teams must maintain engagement with
communities (95) even in the absence of an emergency. Priming coordination systems in
advance helps to prepare responders and communities to work together when disease
outbreaks occur, and to effectively harmonize messages, approaches and the dissemination of
information in a community. Community engagement involves building long-term relationships
between outbreak responders and the community, co-designing solutions and fostering
dialogue to understand the community’s needs.

### Collect, analyse and use social and behavioural data and evidence

Policy and RCCE during an epidemic or pandemic must be informed by social and behavioural
evidence to allow for better understanding of the capacities, expectations and concerns of the
public. Social and behavioural data and evidence are important in identifying populations most
at risk, and making assessments of what people know and believe. This in turn helps to develop
PHSM that align with people’s needs and priorities, and to inform the design and adaptation of
communication content.

### Rapidly communicate risks and adapt recommendations to the local context

Communication approaches during an epidemic or pandemic should emphasize information
sharing and community problem-solving to help people identify actionable ways of protecting
themselves and those around them. Communication materials on risks and protective
behaviours need to be developed and adapted for different audiences and shared through
appropriate and trusted channels for that specific audience. Messages that raise awareness,
encourage health care seeking behaviour and explain uncertainty need to take into account the
individual and collective capacities within a community to act on the advice being given. The
recommended behaviours must be practical and adapted to the local context to ensure their
acceptability and promote their wide adoption. There is also a need to ensure that marginalized
groups (for example, those living in inadequate or overcrowded housing, and minorities, people
with disabilities and indigenous communities) are also engaged in prevention and protection

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 35 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 34 -->

16
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
efforts, have access to information (for example, by making it available in minority languages)
and are able to act upon such information. This involves explaining why practising certain
behaviours is important. Transparency in sharing information and the underlying rationale helps
to build trust and increases the likelihood of cooperation.

### Address infodemics though community feedback mechanisms and social listening,


### and counter misinformation with accurate health information

People and communities generally make health decisions based on their knowledge, personal
beliefs, preferences and circumstances. Feedback mechanisms allow community members to
provide information on their views, concerns and experiences (96). Social listening, both online
and offline, is also essential for understanding the information environment and how it affects
health behaviours. It is important to actively seek feedback from different groups, recognize that
communities are not homogeneous and where appropriate adjust PHSM interventions based on
community input.

| 2. Guideline development process and methods

## 2. Guideline development


## process and methods


### 2.1 Contributors to the process

This guideline was developed based on the combined contributions of a WHO steering group,
evidence review team, guideline methodologist, GDG and External Review Group, in accordance
with the WHO handbook for guideline development (15). Efforts were made to ensure a broad
range of expertise, gender balance and geographical representation across all contributing groups.
Details of all non-WHO contributors and their declarations of interests can be found in Annex 2.

### 2.2 Guideline development steps

This document was developed using the standard procedures described in the WHO handbook
for guideline development (15) and meets international standards for evidence-based guidelines.
In summary, the development process included: (i) defining priority PHSM and developing key
questions; (ii) evidence retrieval; (iii) assessment and synthesis of the evidence; (iv) formulation
of the recommendations; and (v) planning for the dissemination, implementation, impact
evaluation and updating of the guideline.

#### 2.2.1 Defining priority PHSM and developing key questions

The WHO steering group together with the evidence review team developed a list of PHSM
which included the measures reviewed in the 2019 WHO guidance and others implemented
during the COVID-19 pandemic. A subset of PHSM were deemed to be of high priority for review,
taking in account the experiences of the COVID-19 pandemic and previous influenza pandemics.
Whenever possible, the key questions were formulated following the recommended PICO
(population, intervention, comparison, outcomes) model. These key questions then served as the
basis for independent systematic reviews of the literature.

#### 2.2.2 Identifying, appraising and synthesizing evidence

A rigorous systematic review process was conducted by the evidence review team. Evidence
was identified, synthesized and presented in a comprehensive and unbiased manner following
the process outlined in the WHO handbook for guideline development (15). A systematic review
based on selected database search terms was conducted for each of the 20 priority PHSM using
MEDLINE, PubMed, EMBASE and the Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials (CENTRAL).
No language restriction was applied in the search. The systematic review explored the evidence
base on the effectiveness of each PHSM as determined by transmission-related outcomes
(97). Specific such outcomes included reducing transmission, total number of infections,

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 37 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 36 -->

18
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
hospitalizations and deaths, and did not include outcomes such as economic, social and human
rights impacts. The effectiveness of each intervention was compared to a period of influenza (or
other respiratory viruses) circulation where no intervention was implemented.
This review process, which updates the review process conducted to develop the 2019 WHO
guidance, included additional reviews on new PHSM that had been widely implemented
during the COVID-19 pandemic. These new PHSM included air purification, mass testing, indoor
hospitality and recreation measures, stay-at-home provisions and upon-arrival quarantine.
For each PHSM, reviewers first determined whether any relevant systematic review had been
published on its effectiveness within the past five years. If there was a recently published review,
a systematic review limited to the studies published more recently than the latest review (starting
from one year prior to the publication date) was conducted and provided an update to the 2019
review. If a published systematic review of the PHSM within the past five years was not identified,
a full systematic review of the PHSM, including all articles published without limitations on
publication dates was conducted.
The GRADE (16, 17) approach (Table 5) was used to rate the certainty of the evidence for each
PHSM, based on the question of whether the PHSM could reduce influenza transmission in
the community. For each included study, the risk of bias was assessed as part of the GRADE
certainty of evidence evaluation. In general, randomized controlled trials (RCTs) provided the
highest certainty of evidence, followed by observational studies and then simulation studies.
The certainty of evidence may also be upgraded or downgraded based on consideration of
study design limitations (risk of bias), inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision of results, and
publication bias. The final drafts of each review were circulated to the GDG prior to the guideline
development meeting.
Table 5. GRADE certainty of evidence rating
Certainty level
Definition
High
We are very confident that the true effect lies close to that of the estimate of
the effect.
Moderate
We are moderately confident in the effect estimate: the true effect is likely
to be close to the estimate of the effect, but there is a possibility that it is
substantially different.
Low
Our confidence in the effect estimate is limited: the true effect may be
substantially different from the estimate of the effect.
Very low
We have very little confidence in the effect estimate: the true effect is likely to
be substantially different from the estimate of effect.

#### 2.2.3 Formulating recommendations and GDG decision-making

The GDG formulated the recommendations during a 3-day meeting held in Geneva, Switzerland
on 29–31 August 2023. Prior to this meeting, preparatory teleconferences were held for
each PHSM category along with a WHO technical consultation in China, Hong Kong Special
Administrative Region on 27–28 June 2023 to gather the experiences of international experts on
different aspects of PHSM implementation (98).

## 3. Implementation


## considerations

Each of the recommendations provided in the current document was developed based on
the systematic review of supporting evidence for its epidemiological effectiveness and on the
literature regarding associated factors such as economic, social and cost benefits and harms.
The recommendations are intended to support countries in the developing of national pandemic
preparedness plans, and to guide national authorities in the implementation, adaptation,
monitoring and evaluation of PHSM during a severe influenza epidemic or pandemic. It should
be noted that PHSM decision-making is a cyclical process that will include risk assessments
and situation analyses, and implementation monitoring and adjustment (that is, scaling PHSM
up or down) according to the evolving circumstances. To ensure context-specific, equitable
and effective implementation of the recommendations set out in this guideline, multisectoral
collaboration, the involvement of communities, and consideration of other aspects of the national
response will be crucial. The successful implementation and effectiveness of PHSM will largely
depend on contextual factors such as the local transmission rate, population density, health-care
infrastructure, cultural norms, socioeconomic aspects and the extent of societal inequality. In
all cases, adequate consideration should be given to each of the aspects set out below before
any PHSM is implemented or adjusted. WHO guidance and tools are available to support
countries in strengthening systematic, context-specific and evidence-informed decision-making
that maximize health benefits of PHSM while reducing unintended negative consequences for
individuals, societies and economies during outbreaks with epidemic and pandemic potential (11)
and influenza-specific tools will be develop to facilitate the implementation of this guideline.

### 3.1 Prevention, suppression and mitigation

PHSM can be used to prevent (by delaying virus introduction into the country or reducing the
number of cases in the community to zero), to suppress (by reducing the number of infections in
the community) or to mitigate impact (for example, by avoiding overwhelming the health-care
system) (99). The primary goals of the PHSM recommended in this guideline are to suppress
or mitigate an epidemic or pandemic influenza virus, and thus minimize morbidity and
mortality before pharmaceutical therapeutics and vaccines become widely available. While
it is theoretically possible to prevent the introduction of a virus (or achieve zero community
transmission) through the stringent application of some PHSM, this can be highly disruptive to
both society and the economy and can lead to widespread violation of human rights, and is
therefore unlikely to be acceptable and sustainable over the longer term.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 40 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 39 -->

21
| 3. Implementation considerations

### 3.2 Complementary effects of PHSM

The evidence base for this guideline mainly comprised observational evidence that estimated
the effectiveness of numerous measures implemented in a setting at any one time during
the study period. In certain instances, it was challenging to disaggregate the effects of each
individual PHSM. However, any single PHSM is likely to have only a small effect in reducing the
transmission of influenza viruses, and should therefore be considered as one of a package of
measures that will contribute to overall effectiveness.
Determining the optimal timing and duration of implementation is also challenging. In general,
the early implementation of PHSM can be more effective at reducing the impact of influenza
epidemics and pandemics. However, measures with large unintended negative consequences
will be difficult to maintain for long periods. Moreover, if PHSM are implemented too early,
are not feasible, or their rationale is not well defined and communicated, a sense of “fatigue”
and loss of trust may emerge among the population, limiting adherence and ultimately the
effectiveness of the measures. Consideration should also be given to the timing and strategy
for relaxing measures. Furthermore, the PHSM for which recommendations were formulated in
this guideline are not exhaustive, and focus has been given to measures that specifically aim to
reduce the transmission of influenza or other respiratory viruses in the community. There may
therefore be other complementary measures available that can alleviate the adverse effects of
such PHSM, while other specific measures may usefully be applied in settings such as health and
social care facilities.

### 3.3 Acceptability

The effectiveness of PHSM is highly dependent on their acceptability and uptake by individual
and institutional stakeholders. Therefore, the selection and implementation of individual PHSM
as part of the package of measures to be taken should be guided by sufficient considerations of
the local culture, values and preferences, the existing infrastructure and sociopolitical structures.

### 3.4 Equity and human rights

Without careful attention, the implementation of PHSM can exacerbate existing inequalities and
undermine human rights. Equity considerations, and the following human rights norms and
principles should be used guide PHSM design and implementation:
•
The right to life
•
The right to health (availability, accessibility, acceptability and quality)4
•
Equality and non-discrimination
•
The right to the benefits of scientific progress and its implications
•
The right to privacy and confidentiality
•

## 4. Personal protection


## measures


### 4.1 Hand hygiene


### Strong recommendation for – moderate certainty evidence

WHO recommends performing hand hygiene to reduce the transmission of influenza viruses in
community settings during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Remarks

•
Hand hygiene is considered to be a standard precaution shown to reduce the spread of
other infectious diseases. Although the published scientific evidence from RCTs on the
efficacy of hand hygiene alone in reducing laboratory-confirmed influenza virus infections
remains only moderate, it is an important intervention in reducing the risk of other common
infectious diseases. Good hand hygiene should be recommended regardless of influenza
activity in the community due to its general effectiveness in preventing infectious disease
transmission through indirect contact with contaminated surfaces or objects.
•
The effectiveness of hand hygiene increases when used alongside other measures.
•
No evidence was found to support the superior effectiveness of one type of hand hygiene
method over another in reducing influenza virus transmission. However, handwashing with
soap and water is preferred in the case of visibly soiled hands – in circumstances where this
is not possible or feasible, alcohol-based handrub (ABHR) can be used (3).
•
Gloves are used in medical settings but there is insufficient evidence to support the regular
use of gloves as a preventive measure for influenza epidemics and pandemics for the
general population.

### Summary of evidence

Hand hygiene is an essential community-based measure for preventing the transmission of
many infectious diseases. It is a general term referring to any action of hand cleansing to
remove, inactivate or neutralize pathogens using soap or other substances, such as ABHR (103).
Influenza viruses can survive for a short time on human hands and can be transmitted from
contaminated surfaces to hands, supporting the potential for indirect contact transmission to
occur (104–106). Hand hygiene can inactivate or reduce influenza viruses on human hands
(107–109) and thus in theory prevent their indirect contact or fomite transmission.
Thirteen articles describing 12 RCTs on hand hygiene and influenza transmission were included
in the systematic review, and a meta-analysis undertaken for 11 of these studies that had each
included more than 22 000 participants (110–122). In the studies that examined the effectiveness

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 44 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 43 -->

25
| 4. Personal protection measures
of hand hygiene together with face masks, no statistically significant protective effects were
observed in household, school and residential hall settings (110–115). RCTs conducted in
elementary school settings indicated varied effects (116, 117, 122). The efficacy of hand hygiene
in household settings – with or without face mask use – was not significant (112–115, 118, 119).
Several trials reported that poor adherence to hand hygiene might have contributed to the low
efficacies observed (112–114).
Other evidence from outside the above review included the finding of one trial that handwashing
with soap compared to no intervention significantly reduced the incidence of any acute
respiratory infection (123). Another systematic review of observational studies found that among
nine studies assessing hand hygiene practices in the community setting, six had observed
reductions in laboratory-confirmed influenza or possible influenza infections (124). Influenza
transmission events were also estimated in a case-control study carried out at the household
level, where participants self-reported their handwashing frequency. It was estimated that
handwashing three or more times a day significantly reduced the risk of household transmission
of 2009 pandemic influenza A(H1N1) (125).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The systematic review did not identify any RCTs that quantified the effectiveness of hand hygiene
in reducing SARS-CoV-2 transmission. Seven observational studies on the effectiveness of hand
hygiene in reducing laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infections were identified outside the
review (126–132). Four of these studies identified risk factors for SARS-CoV-2 acquisition among
the general public, and found that frequent use of ABHR or hand washing was associated with
lower odds of infection (126, 128, 129, 131). The other three studies identified risk factors for SARS-
CoV-2 transmission in community and household settings and found that frequent handwashing
was associated with a lower risk for SARS-CoV-2 infection (127, 130, 132).
4.1.1
Justification and summary of considerations of GDG members
The moderate certainty of evidence and the absence of harm when implementing hand
hygiene supported the decision of the GDG for a strong recommendation. The members
judged the intervention to be in accordance with universal human rights standards while
equity considerations in the context of performing hand hygiene were judged to vary, given the
known differential access to clean water and hand hygiene products in different settings. The
GDG found hand hygiene to be generally a cost-effective intervention in health-care settings
though financial implications and affordability in community settings could vary. Overall, the
GDG considered performing of hand hygiene to be highly feasible in most settings since public
campaigns have been already conducted in most countries to reduce the transmission of other
common communicable diseases. The members debated about socio-cultural acceptability
of the intervention and thought that most communities would understand the importance
and effectiveness of hand hygiene in preventing common infections and would agree with
the concept of encouraging hand hygiene to prevent the transmission of infectious diseases.
The GDG noted that adherence to proper and effective hand hygiene practices may be low in
general given the difficulty in ensuring the adoption and sustaining of substantial behavioural
changes. However, communication strategies aimed at informing the public on the benefit of the
intervention could improve adherence in the general population.

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
It is challenging to test the efficacy of hand hygiene in RCTs, partly because it can be unethical
to recommend comparison groups to stop washing their hands. Therefore, the intervention in
RCTs is typically based on increasing the frequency of hand hygiene actions or on non-inferiority
trials focusing on specific products (for example, ABHR use in combination with handwashing
versus handwashing alone), making it difficult to estimate the efficacy of hand hygiene alone.
In this context, existing hand hygiene study findings are of moderate certainty overall, and do
not provide strong evidence that increased handwashing or other hand hygiene modalities are
highly effective in reducing influenza virus transmission. However, several experimental studies
(107–109, 133) have provided evidence that hand hygiene can inactivate or remove influenza virus
particles from human hands, and therefore reduce transmission. Observational studies using
data from self-reported hand hygiene practices and increased frequency of handwashing have
also found reductions in influenza infection (124). In addition, four observational studies on the
effectiveness of hand hygiene in reducing laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infections found
important infectious diseases, particularly diarrhoeal diseases (134). There is also evidence that
hand hygiene can reduce the incidence of respiratory illnesses – though evidence of its effect in
reducing laboratory-confirmed influenza is lacking. Hand hygiene is most commonly performed
with water and soap, especially in case of visibly soiled hands, although ABHR provides another

common infections and would agree with the concept of encouraging hand hygiene to prevent
preventing the transmission of diarrhoeal diseases and acute respiratory illnesses (123, 134, 135),
thus helping to reduce the overall burden on health-care systems. The GDG concluded that it
is possible that major improvements in hand hygiene adherence might reduce influenza virus
transmission. There are no known adverse effects when performing hand hygiene other than
Hand hygiene is one of the most cost-effective measures for preventing infections in health-care
settings (137). It is also an important component of general hygiene campaigns in communities
and can reduce the incidence of a variety of infections, and thus reduce associated morbidity
locations, where clean running water, soap or ABHR may not be available, alternative traditional

| 4. Personal protection measures

### Equity and human rights

Equity considerations in the context of performing health hygiene were judged to vary by the
GDG and include the known differential access to clean water and hand hygiene products
in different settings. People living in informal settlements, detention settings, indigenous
populations, refugees and internally displaced people are among those lacking such access (139).
However, the promotion and facilitation of hand hygiene, including through the provision of
water, soap, washing facilities and/or ABHR to communities that lack them (140), could help
to increase equity. Some individuals may also be reluctant to use ABHR on religious grounds.
However, where the use of alcohol as a medicinal agent is permitted in such cases, improving
awareness of the health dimension of ABHR use may help to increase acceptance (141).

### Acceptability

Given its limited cost and broader impact on preventing infections, hand hygiene would seem
to be a very acceptable intervention. However, the GDG noted that compliance and adherence
to proper and effective hand hygiene practices may be low in general given the difficulty in
ensuring the adoption and sustaining of substantial behavioural changes.

### Feasibility

No research was identified regarding the feasibility of implementing hand hygiene. Nevertheless,
the GDG considered this intervention to be highly feasible in most settings as many countries
have already conducted public campaigns to reduce the transmission of communicable diseases
through the performing of hand hygiene.

#### 4.1.2 Knowledge gaps

•
There are important knowledge gaps regarding the mechanisms of human-to-human
transmission of influenza viruses, including the importance of direct and indirect contact,
the degree of viral contamination on hands and various types of surfaces in different
settings, and the potential for contact transmission to occur in different locations and under
different environmental conditions. Additional research on how best to increase hand
hygiene adherence would also be valuable.
•
There is little information on whether greater reductions in virus transmission could be
possible using other interventions in combination with hand hygiene (for example, hand
hygiene combined with mask wearing and isolation of infected individuals from other
household members) and more research is required.

#### 4.1.3 Implementation considerations

•
Education campaigns on effective hand hygiene practices might be needed to address
misinformation and myths, and to improve compliance and adherence in the general
population.
•
Consideration should be given to subgroups that are more vulnerable to infection, and on
the importance of different groups in the community transmission of influenza.
•
To help address issues of equality and ensure non-discrimination in relation to the right
to health, accurate and helpful information, including on the use of alternative traditional
methods for hand hygiene, should be provided.

<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 46 -->

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### 4.2 Mask wearing


### Conditional recommendation for – moderate certainty evidence

WHO suggests that:
•
individuals who are symptomatic and/or have tested positive for influenza wear masks
at all times when in contact with other individuals to reduce the spread of influenza to
others; and
•
all individuals in the community wear masks in crowded indoor settings to potentially
reduce the spread of influenza in such settings during influenza epidemics and pandemics
of high or extraordinary severity.

### Summary of evidence

The literature review included studies that provided evidence on the effectiveness of mask
wearing in reducing influenza virus transmission in the community (that is, settings outside
the health-care system). In particular, twelve RCTs, involving a total of more than 20 000
participants, were identified and a meta-analysis conducted to quantify the effectiveness of
mask wearing in the community (110–115, 118, 142–146). All studies assessed the effectiveness of
medical masks, and one study also had an intervention group using P2 face respirators. Ten
studies had asked every participant in the mask intervention group to wear a medical mask.
One study had enrolled families in which one person had laboratory-confirmed influenza,
and only required the household contacts to wear a medical mask or P2 face respirator (143).
In the remaining study, ill hospital patients were required to wear medical masks to evaluate
their protective effect through source control (144). In the pooled analysis, the point estimates
suggested a relative risk reduction in laboratory-confirmed influenza of 12% among the maskwearing group (risk ratio (RR) = 0.88; 95% confidence interval (CI) = 0.61–1.27; I2 = 22%; P = 0.25)
and a reduction of 10% in the mask-wearing group regardless of whether or not hand hygiene
was also enhanced (RR = 0.90; 95% CI = 0.68–1.18; I2 = 22%; P = 0.23). However, the evidence was
insufficient to exclude chance as an explanation for the reduced risk of transmission.

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The systematic review identified one RCT that had quantified the effectiveness of face masks
in reducing SARS-CoV-2 infections during the early stage of the pandemic (145). This study had
found that community-based face mask usage in Denmark did not reduce laboratory-confirmed
SARS-CoV-2 infections in the masked group. To date, an RCT carried out in Bangladesh has
been the largest trial assessing the effectiveness of face masks in reducing SARS-CoV-2
infections. However, this study was not included in the systematic review because the outcome
was not laboratory-confirmed infection. Study results indicated a 9.5% reduction in symptomatic
seroprevalence for SARS-CoV-2 among surgical mask wearers in rural areas of Bangladesh (147).
Another review carried out earlier in the COVID-19 pandemic had included observational studies
and two RCTs, and found evidence of a 6–15% reduction in the risk of influenza-like illness (ILI)
associated with the use of face masks in the community setting (148). Another review based
on 39 studies estimated that the wearing of face masks had reduced the risk of infection with
SARS-CoV-2 or other betacoronaviruses by 14.3% (95% CI = 10.7–15.9) (149).
