### 4.3 Respiratory hygiene and cough etiquette


### Strong recommendation for – moderate certainty evidence

WHO recommends the practising of respiratory hygiene and cough etiquette by individuals
not wearing a mask to prevent transmission of influenza viruses during influenza epidemics
and pandemics.

### Summary of evidence

The systematic review identified one RCT that studied the effectiveness of using ABHR
and respiratory hygiene education classes among school-aged children compared to no
intervention. A statistically significant reduction was observed in the number of laboratoryconfirmed influenza cases among the students from the 12 schools that had received the
intervention (122). Another study, not captured in the current review, reported a reduction in selfreported respiratory illnesses associated with the practising of cough etiquette among American
pilgrims to the 2009 Hajj (159). However, another observational study reported that respiratory
hygiene was not effective in blocking the release of droplets during coughing (160).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

No study was identified that quantified the effectiveness of respiratory hygiene and cough
etiquette in reducing the number of laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infections.

#### 4.3.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The moderate certainty evidence and the absence of harm when implementing respiratory
hygiene and cough etiquette supported the decision of the GDG for a strong recommendation.
The members judged the intervention to be in accordance with universal human rights
standards. Overall, the GDG did not identify any major concerns in terms of resource
implications, equity, acceptability and feasibility of respiratory hygiene and cough etiquette.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is moderate certainty evidence indicating that respiratory hygiene and cough
etiquette combined with hand hygiene has a beneficial effect in reducing the incidence of
laboratory-confirmed influenza cases.

### Values and preferences

Respiratory hygiene and cough etiquette is recognized as important in many communities.
Improvements to respiratory hygiene in communities could also prevent the spread of respiratory
infections other than influenza.

### Balance of benefits and harms

There are no anticipated harms associated with the practising of respiratory hygiene and
cough etiquette.

| 5. Environmental measures

### 5.2 Modifying indoor humidity


### Strong recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

WHO recommends against modifying indoor humidity to reduce the transmission of influenza
viruses during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Summary of evidence

Increased humidity has been correlated with reduced influenza virus transmission in cold
and dry climates (170, 171), while very high humidity has been associated with increased virus
transmission in hot and humid climates (22). In one study identified in the systematic review, the
humidity in two preschool classrooms at a community school in the USA was modified using an
electrode steam humidifier with steam blower (171). Samples taken from paper-wrapped fomites
handled by students (such as marker pens and wooden toy blocks) and from the air were tested
for influenza A using PCR. The study noted that the number of positive tests for influenza A was
lower for both fomites (odds ratio (OR) = 0.51; 95% CI =0.33–0.78) and air samples (OR = 0.51;
95% CI = 0.29–0.89) in the humidified classrooms compared to the control classrooms. Results
also indicated that there were 2.3 times as many ILI cases in the control classrooms compared to
the humidified classrooms. However, whether or not this indicates a causal relationship between
the number of such cases and the levels of influenza viruses detected in the rooms is unknown.
Two intervention studies, not included in the current review, have investigated the modification
of humidity in the environment and found that a reduction in absolute humidity has been
associated with influenza outbreaks. For example, one prospective cohort study found that
lower indoor absolute humidity in individual households was associated with a higher risk of
acute respiratory infection in community-dwelling older adults in Hong Kong SAR, China during
cold seasons (172). A second study conducted in eight schools in China reported no association
between relative humidity and self-reported respiratory infections. Positive associations were
observed between indoor relative humidity in schools and the onset of daytime attacks of
breathlessness, nocturnal cough and nocturnal wheeze or breathlessness (173).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

No studies were identified that quantified the effectiveness of modifying indoor humidity in
reducing laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infections.

#### 5.2.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The limited availability and the very low certainty evidence caused the GDG to conclude with
a strong recommendation against modifying indoor humidity. Members judged the balance
of health benefit and harms to be variable and alignment with human rights as probable.
The GDG discussed the financial and economic considerations and found to probably favor
the comparison given that the costs associated to the purchase, usage and maintenance of
humidifiers could not be affordable in several settings. The members valued the acceptability
of key stakeholders as uncertain and expressed concerns about the feasibility of this
measure overall.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 57 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 56 -->

38
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence that modifying indoor humidity has an effect on
reducing the transmission of influenza viruses.

### Values and preferences

Values and preferences with regard to modifying indoor humidity are uncertain and are likely
subject to individual preferences for ambient humidity. This measure has yet to be implemented
on a large scale and there are few intervention studies.

### Balance of benefits and harms

Humidification may increase the growth of mould and mildew, which could cause health
problems (174). According to WHO, indoor dampness or mould creates a considerable health
burden (for example, asthma) in children (175). The GDG expressed concerns about potential
safety issues related to the implementation of this measure.

### Resource implications

Humidifiers may not be affordable to purchase and can be costly to run and maintain. The GDG
also expressed concerns about the cost of implementation of this measure.

### Equity and human rights

Increasing moisture levels in the air may adversely impact the health of some individuals,
particularly those who may be more vulnerable. Those with asthma often experience problems
in very cold or hot temperatures, which can increase airway irritation. Mild temperatures and
low levels of humidity might decrease the risk of such irritation.

### Acceptability

Modifying humidity is likely to be acceptable, but subject to individual preferences for
ambient humidity.

### Feasibility

During an outbreak, there may be insufficient availability of humidifiers at short notice, and it
may not be logistically feasible to humidify all buildings in a community. Modifying humidity in
indoor community settings (such as classrooms, offices or households) has yet to be implemented
on a large scale. The GDG expressed concerns about the feasibility of this measure.

#### 5.2.2 Knowledge gaps

•
The precise biological mechanism by which humidity affects the survival of influenza
viruses is unclear (170, 171). Many studies have looked at this effect under laboratory
conditions but very few have tested it in real-life settings. RCTs would provide more
compelling evidence with regard to the effectiveness of this measure in reducing
influenza virus transmission.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 58 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 57 -->

39
| 5. Environmental measures

### 5.3 Surface and object cleaning and


## disinfection


### Conditional recommendation for – low certainty evidence

WHO suggests surface and object cleaning and disinfection to reduce the transmission of
influenza viruses during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Summary of evidence

Four studies were included in the systematic review of the effectiveness of surface and object
cleaning and disinfection in reducing influenza transmission (176–179). An RCT of the disinfection
of toys and linen in daycare facilities in Denmark found reduced detection of some respiratory
viruses in the environment but no statistically significant effect on laboratory-confirmed influenza
or acute respiratory illness among children in the post-intervention period (176). Another RCT
conducted in an elementary school in the USA reported that surface disinfection combined with
hand hygiene could reduce absenteeism due to gastrointestinal illness, but not absenteeism
due to respiratory illness, among students (177). A cross-sectional study in school-age children
in Spain, Netherlands (Kingdom of the) and Finland showed that passive exposure to sodium
hypochlorite (bleach) in households was significantly associated with an increase in the rate
of parent-reported influenza infections. It was hypothesized that this could be related to the
irritant properties of bleach (178). A cluster RCT conducted in primary schools in the Lao People’s
Democratic Republic reported that the receipt of water supply, sanitation and handwashing
facilities, drinking water filters, and behaviour change education and promotion had no impact
on self-reported symptoms of respiratory infection in participating schools (179).
Influenza viruses can survive on surfaces and objects for at least several hours and up to
one week (104, 105, 180–182). Although influenza virus RNA has also been detected in various
community settings, it is not usually viable (176, 183–187). Surface and object cleaning and
disinfection can be effective at inactivating or reducing viable influenza viruses on surfaces
(188–190). Theoretically, surface and object cleaning and disinfection could thus prevent the
indirect contact transmission of influenza.

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

Two studies included in the systematic review attempted to quantify cleaning and disinfection
frequency against SARS-CoV-2 transmission (191, 192). One of these studies was a retrospective
cohort study conducted in Beijing, China that reported that the use of chlorine- or ethanolbased disinfectant in households was associated with a reduced risk of confirmed SARS-CoV-2
secondary household transmission (191). However, a cross-sectional study that surveyed on-site
camps in the USA reported that increased cleaning frequency was not associated with a reduced
risk of SARS-CoV-2 infection among campers or staff (192).

#### 5.3.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The GDG decision for conditional recommendation for surface and object cleaning and
disinfection was supported by the low certainty of evidence and the probably positive balance
between desirable and undesirable health effects of the intervention. Nevertheless, the members

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 59 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 58 -->

40
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
noted the potential negative impact related to the safety of some cleaning products. The
members judged the intervention to be in accordance with universal human rights standards.
Overall, the GDG did not identify any major concerns in terms of resource implications, equity,
acceptability and feasibility although these considerations could vary across different setting.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is low certainty evidence that surface and object cleaning and disinfection can
reduce laboratory-confirmed cases of influenza virus infection. However, experimental studies
have suggested that surface and object cleaning and disinfection could effectively inactivate
or reduce viable influenza viruses on surfaces. Due to mechanistic plausibility, this intervention
could reduce the indirect contact transmission of such viruses.

### Values and preferences

A telephone survey conducted in Europe found that 82% of respondents believed that cleaning
or disinfecting objects might reduce the risk of influenza infections (193). Environmental cleaning
and disinfection is also a common strategy for reducing the risk of a variety of infections.

### Balance of benefits and harms

Cleaning with detergent-based cleaners and disinfecting with bleach can inactivate or remove
influenza viruses on surfaces and objects, and could in theory reduce influenza transmission.
However, most disinfectants (such as bleach) require a pre-cleaning step before the disinfectant
is applied (194). In addition, the incorrect use of disinfectants, including poor ventilation during
their use, can be harmful (7).

### Resource implications

The cost of disinfectants is relatively low and/or affordable in many locations. However, high
demand for such agents during a pandemic can drive costs up.

### Equity and human rights

Cleaning product selection can be an issue as some disinfectants are irritants and may lead
to adverse effects in some groups (178). Alcohol-based disinfectants may not be universally
accepted in the context of the prohibition on alcohol consumption under some religions, including
Islam (141). However, most countries have no legislation restricting the use of alcohol as an
ingredient in household cleaning agents, and in Muslim tradition, alcohol is permitted as a
cleansing ingredient (141). The safety of cleaning personnel should always be considered when
making recommendations for surface and object cleaning using disinfectants.

### Acceptability

This intervention is accepted by policy-makers and health-care workers worldwide. However, its
acceptability in the general population may vary across different countries.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 60 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 59 -->

41
| 5. Environmental measures

### Feasibility

This intervention is considered to be feasible, with both cleaning and disinfection products readily
available from a variety of sources, such as supermarkets and convenience stores.

#### 5.3.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Touching contaminated surfaces and subsequently transferring respiratory viruses to the
nose, mouth or eyes through the hands is believed to be a route of transmission but its
relative importance compared to other transmission routes is unclear, and is likely smaller
than close contact with infected individuals.
•
Evidence on the effectiveness of surface and object cleaning and disinfection in reducing
influenza virus transmission is limited. More trials are needed to study the effect of
this measure.
•
Studies in which the outcome is laboratory-confirmed influenza, rather than acute
respiratory infection, would provide the best evidence on effectiveness.
•
Studies in various settings (for example, households, schools, workplaces and public places),
along with investigation of the different types and concentrations of cleaning products
used, and how often, are also needed.

#### 5.3.3 Implementation considerations

•
Cleaning and disinfection practices are important to reduce the potential for influenza virus
contamination in community settings, such as the home, offices, schools, gyms, publicly
accessible buildings, faith-based community centres, markets, transportation settings,
restaurants and other workplaces.
•
High-touch surfaces in all such settings should be identified for priority disinfection.
•
Disinfection should be carried out using products such as sodium hypochlorite (bleach) or
alcohol at the recommended concentrations.
•
Surfaces must first be cleaned with water and soap, or a detergent, to remove dirt prior
to disinfection.
•
Disinfectant spraying or fumigation, either outdoors or in large indoor areas (such
as meeting rooms or classrooms), are not recommended due to their likely lack of
effectiveness, possible damage to the environment and the potential exposure of humans
and animals to irritant chemicals (195).

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 61 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 60 -->

42
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### 5.4 Ventilation


### Strong recommendation for – low certainty evidence

WHO recommends increasing mechanical or natural ventilation in both households and nonresidential buildings to reduce the transmission of influenza viruses during influenza epidemics
and pandemics.

### Summary of evidence

Three studies were included in the systematic review (196–198). Of these, one cohort study
compared two college residential hall buildings in Maryland, USA using multi-zone airflow
modelling and swab-based testing. A lower incidence of laboratory-confirmed respiratory
virus infections (including influenza A and B, human coronavirus and other common respiratory
viruses) was observed in the building that had a dedicated outdoor air system compared to the
building with air conditioning but no outdoor air supply (196). A cross-sectional study conducted
in a Chinese university also indicated that students living in rooms with lower ventilation rates
were associated with a higher incidence of self-reported common colds and influenza (197). A
community-based case-control study in Southern Ethiopia found that the probability of acute
respiratory infection was four times higher in children who lived in poorly ventilated houses
compared to those who lived in houses with good ventilation (198).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

Only one study was identified that quantified the effectiveness of increased ventilation in
preventing SARS-CoV-2 infection (199). This internet-based nationwide prospective cohort
study evaluated the effectiveness of preventive behaviours against self-reported COVID-19like symptoms among working adults in Japan. Significantly higher levels of all COVID-19-like
symptoms except fever were observed in participants who reported rarely or never opening
windows and doors during the study period (199).

#### 5.4.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The GDG decision on strong recommendation for increasing ventilation was supported by the
low certainty evidence and the likely absence of major harms among the general population
when implementing this measure. Members acknowledged the importance of ventilation as a
key strategy for infection prevention and control in health-care setting as well as in community
settings. The GDG judged the intervention to probably be in accordance with universal human
rights standards. Overall, the GDG did not identify any major concerns in terms of resource
implications, equity, acceptability and feasibility although these considerations may vary across
different setting.

### Certainty of the evidence

Despite the above findings on the beneficial effects of good ventilation in reducing respiratory
virus transmission, there is low certainty evidence overall that introducing mechanical ventilation
or increasing the air flow rate to non-residential buildings and increasing the natural ventilation
of households would reduce influenza virus transmission. However, there is mechanistic support
for the potentially beneficial effect of this measure given that increasing indoor ventilation

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 62 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 61 -->

43
| 5. Environmental measures
rate will increase the dilution of infectious particles in the air and thus decrease the risk of
infection transmission (13).

### Values and preferences

Increasing ventilation is a common practice in office buildings (using mechanical ventilation) and
households (by opening windows).

### Balance of benefits and harms

Regions in which vector-borne diseases are prevalent will require consideration to be given to
the use of complementary measures (such as mosquito screens). However, as airflow pattern
and direction are important considerations (200), care must also be taken to avoid such
measures adversely affecting these properties (14). In addition, if the outdoor temperature is
often very low (for example, during winter), the use of increased ventilation may lead to thermal
discomfort for some individuals. There is also a risk of increased exposure to air pollution and
allergens that may trigger asthmatic attacks or allergies if the outdoor air quality is poor.
However, the GDG considered that there is likely to be an absence of major harms among the
general population when implementing increased ventilation measures.

### Resource implications

Increasing indoor ventilation by opening windows is likely to incur minimal additional direct
costs. However, direct costs (both installation and running costs) can arise when implementing
mechanical ventilation for buildings or homes. In cold climates, both increased natural or
mechanical ventilation could result in increased indirect costs (for example, heating costs).

### Equity and human rights

The direct and/or indirect costs of mechanical or natural ventilation could be a barrier to
affordability, which may disproportionately affect certain population groups. The GDG
considered that there were no other major equity and human rights concerns associated with
the use of increased ventilation.

### Acceptability

Increased ventilation does not affect daily activities and may reduce the transmission of
influenza in the community. As a result, the level of acceptability is likely to be high.

### Feasibility

Increased ventilation is likely to be feasible in most settings. However, feasibility may be lower in
crowded or low-income settings.

#### 5.4.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Evidence of the effectiveness of increased indoor ventilation on reducing influenza virus
transmission is limited.
•
RCTs would provide more compelling evidence with regard to the effectiveness of this
measure in reducing influenza virus transmission.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 63 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 62 -->

44
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
•
Improved understanding of how to increase the feasibility of this intervention in some
settings would be helpful.

#### 5.4.3 Implementation considerations

•
Optimal natural and mechanical ventilation requirements adapted to different indoor
settings have been outlined by WHO (14). For example, the minimum recommended
ventilation rate for non-residential buildings is 10 litres per second per person.
•
Implementing proper ventilation systems requires specialist engineering knowledge and
consideration of factors such as energy conservation, prevalence of vector-borne diseases
and outdoor air pollution levels.
•
Consideration should also be given to vulnerable populations (such as those living in
crowded conditions) that do not have access to good ventilation.
•
Increasing natural ventilation by opening the windows may be a valid approach in some
settings with poor ventilation but not necessarily in buildings with adequate ventilation (201).

## 6. Active case-finding and


## contact identification

WHO suggests against contact tracing to reduce the transmission of influenza viruses during
influenza epidemics and pandemics, except for the first cases of an outbreak of an emerging
influenza virus strain in order to collect information on the characteristics of the disease and
The short serial interval of influenza makes contact tracing inefficient, and it is highly
resource intensive. Identifying close contacts would also quickly overwhelm the public
Since no observational evidence was available on the effectiveness of contact tracing in
mitigating the public health impact of influenza, four simulation studies were included in
the systematic review (202–205). Contact tracing was studied in combination with other
interventions such as quarantine, isolation and provision of antiviral drugs. Evidence on the
the combination of contact tracing, quarantine, isolation and antiviral drugs could reduce
the infection attack rate by 40% (202), while another study predicted that it would be difficult
to control influenza spread even with 90% contact tracing and quarantine because of a
of isolation, treatment of cases, contact tracing, quarantine and post-exposure prophylaxis
was predicted to delay the epidemic peak for 6 weeks, assuming a case detection rate
of 30% (205). In addition, it has been suggested that for influenza viruses, the combination of

### Conditional recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

overall effectiveness of contact tracing varied. One simulation model (R0 = 1.8) indicated that
presumed high level of pre-symptomatic or asymptomatic transmission (204). A combination
contact tracing with quarantine would be more effective than contact tracing combined with

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 65 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 64 -->

46
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The systematic review identified seventeen observational studies that estimated the effectiveness
of contact tracing in reducing the transmission of SARS-CoV-2 (206–222). Three of the studies
found a beneficial effect associated with the use of digital contact tracing applications (208, 210,
214). Using an unexpected delay in contact tracing in parts of the United Kingdom compared
to parts that were not affected by the delay, it was estimated that every case referred late to
contact tracing was associated with 18.6 additional infections in a 6-week period (208). Another
study assessed the overall impact of contact tracing apps in the United Kingdom, and estimated
that a total of 594 000 (317 000–914 000) cases had been averted over a 5-month period (214).
Four studies found that contact tracing also shortened the delay between symptom onset and
case isolation (207, 209, 211, 213). Among the remaining studies, one retrospective investigation of
the testing-tracing-treatment response to COVID-19 in the Republic of Korea estimated that due
to prompt tracing, the instantaneous reproduction number had been more than halved during
the study period (from 1.3 to 0.6) (212).
6.1.1
Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members
The evidence identified was limited and was rated of very low certainty. The GDG judged
the balance of health benefit and harms to be variable, noting that there may be a possible
benefit in the early phases of a pandemic for outbreak investigations purposes and for the
characterization of the emerged virus. The GDG discussed the resource implications and found
to favor the comparison given the large amount of economic and human resources required
when contact tracing is implemented on a large scale. Members agreed that contact tracing
might not be cost-effective also considering the short serial interval of influenza. Identifying
close contacts would also quickly overwhelm the public health resources available to manage
them. The GDG debated the implications on equity and human rights, values and preference
and acceptability and recognized that these may vary. They thought that some individuals
may anticipate stigma or privacy-related risks and refuse to be contact traced. The members
expressed concerns about the feasibility of this measure overall valuing contact tracing probably
not feasible in several settings. The very low certainty evidence and these considerations caused
the GDG to conclude with a conditional recommendation against contact tracing.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence that contact tracing has an effect on reducing the
transmission of influenza. All included studies for influenza were simulation models. Evidence
for SARS-CoV-2 estimated a modest effect when combined with follow-up measures such as
isolation and quarantine; however, the individual effect of these measures is not known.

### Values and preferences

There is uncertainty surrounding community values and preferences with regard to the use
of contact tracing for the control of influenza as different populations and cultures may have
different perceptions and value preferences when balancing individual privacy and public health
safety and security. Mandatory contact tracing may cause concerns and uneasiness among
some cases and their contacts.

exposed to respiratory viruses through contact with a confirmed or suspected case. This
intervention can reduce the delay between symptom onset and treatment, as well as the

undue burden on potentially very ill individuals to remember and document their contacts (224).

to digital contact tracing and where appropriate privacy safeguards are not in place and upheld

risks to the right to privacy, can be reduced through the use of digital contact tracing approaches
and (iii) supported by safeguards to prevent the collection of private information for unrelated

as it relies on access to resources and digital technologies, and strategies should be deployed
The acceptability of contact tracing among the public can vary between cultures and populations
depending on overall value preferences in balancing individual privacy and public health safety
countries have noted that the national culture, level of general trust in authorities and individual

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 67 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 66 -->

48
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
factors (such as perceptions of power-distance and individualism) can all influence individual and
collective sentiment towards the implementation of contact tracing initiatives (227, 228).

### Feasibility

Contact tracing requires large numbers of trained personnel and considerable resources (for
example, in telecommunications) and as a result may be less feasible in low- to middle-income
countries in which resources are limited. In addition, the implementation and effectiveness
of contact tracing both rely on the capacity to detect cases, and efforts are likely to be
hampered by the short incubation and infectious periods of influenza (204). The triggers for
activating and deactivating contact tracing for optimal effect in controlling influenza outbreaks
remain unknown.

#### 6.1.2 Knowledge gaps

•
There have been very few studies on the effectiveness of contact tracing in reducing
influenza virus transmission in the community, and none that have studied contact tracing
as a single standalone intervention.
•
Knowledge of the specific impact of contact tracing on disease transmission might be useful
in modifying public behaviour and reducing the frequency of interpersonal contacts in the
community during an outbreak.
•
Currently available studies in community settings are all simulation studies and evidence of
greater strength is needed to provide a more robust understanding of the effectiveness and
value of contact tracing in reducing influenza transmission.
•
Data are also lacking on the impacts of different intensities of contact tracing, and on its
optimal timeframe, feasibility and cost-benefit.
•
Real-world assessment of digital contact tracing as a standalone measure to inform public
health authorities and the public about exposure risk would also be valuable.
•
Investigation of the use of digital contact tracing applications to modify contact patterns in
the community to reduce influenza transmission is also required.

#### 6.1.3 Implementation considerations

•
Contact tracing could be considered where there is sufficient digital (or other) contact
tracing capacity. However, this would need to be implemented alongside sufficient data
protection and human rights safeguards.
•
To the extent possible, when implementing contact tracing, governments should seek prior
and informed consent from the infected individual. Where informed consent is not possible
or advisable, then at a minimum people should be informed about what data will be used,
how and by whom.
•
In the case of low incidence, contact tracing may be considered as part of a suppression
strategy in countries with sufficient contact tracing capacity, testing capacity and
quarantine facilities.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 68 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 67 -->

49
| 6. Active case-finding and contact identification measures

### 6.2 Isolation of symptomatic and/or


## infected individuals


### Strong recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

WHO recommends voluntary home isolation of symptomatic and/or infected individuals in
every circumstance to reduce the transmission of influenza viruses during influenza epidemics
and pandemics.

### Conditional recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

WHO suggests mandatory isolation of symptomatic and/or infected individuals at home or in a
dedicated facility to reduce the transmission of influenza viruses in exceptional circumstances,
such as epidemics and pandemics of high or extraordinary severity.

### Summary of evidence

Isolation is a fundamental public health measure to prevent the transmission of many infectious
diseases. However, the impact of isolation on influenza transmission has rarely been studied
in detail. The systematic review identified four studies that assessed the effectiveness of
isolation on influenza virus transmission (55, 229-231). Two of these reported reductions in the
observed attack rate when isolation was used as part of a package of measures in a physical
training camp and in a residential home for older adults (229, 230). In addition, a review of the
interventions implemented during the 1918 Spanish influenza pandemic noted that excess death
rates due to pneumonia and influenza decreased in New York City and Denver after isolation
and quarantine measures were implemented (55). Another outbreak investigation reported a
reduction in the cumulative incidence of influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 cases on a navy ship that had
implemented an isolation policy (231).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

Eight studies have been identified that assessed the effectiveness of case isolation in reducing
SARS-CoV-2 transmission during the early stages of the COVID-19 pandemic (232–239). Of these,
two studies found an association between case isolation and epidemiological trends suggesting
suppression of disease spread (232, 238). One study compared the difference between countries
implementing isolation measures in health-care facilities and at home, and found that the daily
number of new SARS-CoV-2 cases fell more rapidly in countries with strict isolation policies (233).
Other studies in the review have reported that the reproductive number took longer to fall
below one when cases were isolated according to disease severity rather than universal case
isolation (234, 235).

#### 6.2.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The GDG decision to recommend isolation of symptomatic and/or infected individuals was
supported by very low certainty evidence and the balance between health benefits and harms
favoring the intervention. The members found that evidence on cost-effectiveness of isolation
alone was limited; however, voluntary isolation appeared to be more effective and less costly
when compared to other PHSM, such as school closure. The members agreed that isolation is a

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 69 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 68 -->

50
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
fundamental public health measure to prevent the transmission of several infectious pathogens
and judged voluntary isolation to be in accordance with universal human right for health.
Nevertheless, the GDG noted human rights concerns when isolation measures are mandated,
such as restriction of freedom of movement or access to food. Therefore, the GDG debated and
deliberated for a strong recommendation for voluntary isolation of symptomatic and/or infected
individuals and a conditional recommendation for mandatory isolation to only limit mandate to
exceptional circumstances, such as epidemics and pandemics of high or extraordinary severity.
They highlighted the need for governments and communities to consider the impact of this
intervention on affected individuals and mitigate it through the implementation of social security
policies. Members recognized that values and preferences and acceptability of individuals and
groups assigned to undergo isolation may vary; however, members believed that acceptance of
isolation policies may also depend on the severity of infection, with very severe infections likely
to increase acceptance when linked to timely and adequate care. Overall, the GDG thought
that feasibility of isolation may vary across different setting and be related to the stringency of
the intervention.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence that the isolation of symptomatic and/or infected
individuals substantially affects the transmission of influenza viruses, except perhaps in closed
settings. However, four observational studies reported that isolation was effective at reducing
influenza virus transmission in combination with other PHSM. Eight observational studies
reported that isolation was effective at reducing the transmission of SARS-CoV-2, again in
combination with other PHSM, and suggested that centralized out-of-home isolation may be
more effective than home isolation. One limitation of isolation in terms of reducing influenza
virus transmission is the effect of delays in recognizing symptoms and/or receiving a laboratory
diagnosis, particularly as contagiousness likely peaks around the time of symptom onset, with
some individuals potentially contagious even prior to symptom development (240).

### Values and preferences

There may be variability in the values and preferences of groups assigned to undergo isolation.
Isolation can cause distress through fear and risk perceptions, especially when people face
unclear information and communication during a disease outbreak (241). In addition, health-care
workers and contacts related to those isolated may report social stigma and emotional strain due
to loss of anonymity (242). However, individuals who have no close relationship with isolated cases,
may consider isolation to be an effective intervention in reducing their own chances of becoming
infected (241). Experiences during the COVID-19 pandemic revealed specific concerns around
mandatory isolation policies, such as loss of income or employment. Individual acceptance of
isolation policies may also depend on the severity of infection, with very severe infections likely to
increase acceptance of isolation policies when linked to timely and adequate care.

### Balance of benefits and harms

The objective of isolation is to reduce virus transmission by reducing contact between contagious
and susceptible individuals (6). The overall effectiveness of isolation is likely to be moderate
and likely to be greater when combined with other PHSM. Individuals who live with an isolated
person could be at higher risk of infection during home isolation due to increased contact (243).
Several studies have indicated that countries that implemented more stringent isolation policies
were more successful in reducing the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic (233–235). However,

settings with poor sanitation and high levels of vulnerability, thereby promoting the right to health
of the community at large. However, lower-income and informal employment (such as informal
trading, waste reclamation or sex work) is more likely to require in-person attendance (rather

52
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Acceptability

The isolation of sick individuals is generally widely accepted by policy-makers and health-care
workers, while acceptability and adherence among the public varies. It may become increasingly
less accepted during prolonged periods of isolation or implementation, as evidenced by the
onset of “pandemic fatigue” as the COVID-19 pandemic progressed. One survey conducted
among the parents or guardians of university students in the USA during the 2009 H1N1 influenza
pandemic indicated that a majority of respondents envisaged isolating themselves away from
others should they became ill (258). However, in a separate survey of university students, only
6.4% of respondents reported remaining in home isolation when ill (259). In a review of five
studies, it was reported that 50–96% of respondents intended to stay home rather than go to
work if they became symptomatic. However, in another six studies covered by the same review,
the reported values for individuals who had actually done so were significantly lower (1–26%)
(260). Family structure or the presumed infection status of family members can affect whether
people accept isolation plans; for example, young children are less likely to be isolated alone at
any stage of an epidemic (202).

### Feasibility

The isolation of sick individuals may not be feasible in certain circumstances and there are likely
to at least be obstacles to isolation in some locations. For example, it may not be straightforward
for families of low socioeconomic status or other vulnerable groups to isolate individually at
home, or to even have a home. In addition, infected individuals unaware of their infection
status (for example, pre-symptomatic or asymptomatic) could perpetuate transmission in the
community (7). The effectiveness of case isolation as a PHSM is also sensitive to the response
time, with delays potentially inevitable in many situations (261). In addition, ethical and social
issues related to isolation may contribute to variable acceptability and adherence among
the community.

#### 6.2.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Available epidemiological studies looked at isolation combined with other interventions or
did not use laboratory-confirmed influenza as the outcome of interest. Although it is difficult
to study isolation using RCTs, such studies would be very valuable.
•
The understanding of virus transmission dynamics is incomplete, including the importance
of pre-symptomatic contagiousness for influenza (262) and the proportion of infections that
are asymptomatic (57). The optimal duration of isolation is also unclear. Research to identify
an optimal strategy for the isolation of asymptomatic but potentially contagious individuals
would be valuable.
•
Due to the short incubation and latency period (time from infection to infectiousness) of
influenza viruses, the isolation of sick individuals may not be as effective or feasible as it
would be for other respiratory virus infections with longer incubation and latency periods.
Studies on the feasibility and cost-effectiveness of case isolation measures during influenza
epidemics and pandemics would thus inform decision-making.

#### 6.2.3 Implementation considerations

•
Addressing the practical needs of isolated individuals is crucial, especially for those more
vulnerable, such as people living alone or the elderly. Ensuring access to necessary medical
supplies and adequate food is essential.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 72 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 71 -->

53
| 6. Active case-finding and contact identification measures
•
When healthy individuals live with someone who is isolating, the implementing of personal
protection measures to help reduce the risk of household transmission is very important.
•
The duration of isolation should be guided by the duration of infectiousness of the disease.
Early studies on the course of infectiousness over time can thus inform this decision.
•
Focusing on the initial days when infectiousness is highest and medical attention may be
most needed is essential. Monitoring symptoms during this period will be crucial.
•
During continued community transmission, mandatory isolation could become increasingly
challenging to enforce and may have limited measurable impact on overall mitigation,
whereas voluntary self-isolation should always be part of the strategy.
•
Isolation has significant social and economic consequences for affected individuals.
Governments and communities should consider these impacts and aim to mitigate them
through the implementation of social security policies.
•
For individuals who find it difficult to home isolate, facility-based isolation can be
considered, provided that appropriate care and support is provided in such facilities.
•
In the absence of widely available rapid diagnostic tests for a newly emerged influenza
virus, isolation recommendations may need to rely on symptomatic illness rather than
laboratory results.

#### 6.3.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The evidence identified was limited and was rated of very low certainty. The GDG judged
the balance of health benefit and harms to be variable. The GDG discussed the resource

### Conditional recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

The systematic review did not identify any published evidence on the effect of mass testing on
reducing influenza virus transmission. To date, several commercial RATs for diagnosing seasonal
A total of 22 studies that had assessed the effectiveness of mass testing in reducing the impact of
SARS-CoV-2 were identified in the review (264–271). Twenty studies were observational and two
were modelling studies. Eleven studies from six countries reported on the impact of populationlevel rapid antigen testing as a screening measure (264–274). The other eleven studies reported
on the impact of PCR testing in the community (275–282) or the impact of pooled PCR testing in
schools and universities (283–285). All identified studies showed a benefical effect of mass testing
typically combined with other PHSM such as isolation of infected individuals and quarantine of
their contacts to prevent further transmission. The outcomes of the studies included reduction
implications and found to probably favor the comparison given the large number of economic

such a measure could be implemented if a test with a sufficiently high sensitivity and specificity

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 74 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 73 -->

55
| 6. Active case-finding and contact identification measures
positive to seasonal or pandemic influenza, and this would require PCR confirmation. The GDG
debated the implications on equity and human rights, values and preference and acceptability
and recognized that these may vary and would depend on factors such as the modality, the
frequency or the cost associated to testing. The members judged the feasibility to be variable
across different settings and be dependent by the resources available in a country, the capacity
to test at large scale and to handle positive individuals and their close contacts. The very low
certainty evidence together with these considerations caused the GDG to conclude with a
conditional recommendation against mass testing.

### Certainty of the evidence

Currently there is no evidence on the effect of mass testing on reducing influenza virus
transmission, while the corresponding evidence for SARS-CoV-2 is of very low certainty overall.
All 22 studies estimated the effect of mass testing on SARS-CoV-2 transmission and its impacts,
all of which showed a beneficial effect. Three studies indicated that the use of mass testing
using pooled PCR testing reduced test positivity in schools. Eleven studies suggested a beneficial
impact of population-wide rapid antigen testing, when combined with other PHSM such as
isolation and quarantine.

### Values and preferences

Although mass testing in many locations has met little resistance, concerns could arise if such
tests were mandatory and the consequences of reporting a positive case significant (for
example, isolation of the positive individual and quarantine of close contacts away from their
home). Allowing the voluntary reporting of test results may allay such concerns. One survey
carried out in Indonesia found that upon receiving a positive test, 86% of respondents would
report it, 97% would self-isolate and 90% would warn their close contacts (286).

### Balance of benefits and harms

Reducing the onward transmission of respiratory viruses by quickly identifying cases in the
community, followed by isolation and treatment, is a benefit to society. Even without isolation
or quarantine, individuals could still voluntarily change their behaviour to reduce their own risk
of infection if negative or the risk of transmitting an infection to others if positive (287) – though
this would not be as effective. There are no apparent harms to the health of an individual as
a result of testing, which can instead allow for quick treatment if the test is positive. However,
some tests may have adverse effects, such as discomfort, headaches and nosebleeds resulting
from nasopharyngeal swabs (288). The cost-effectiveness of implementing mass testing should
be weighed against the current or expected prevalence in the community as high positive
predictive values may only be achieved (for example, for RATs) when the prevalence is high.
If the sensitivity and/or specificity of a test are poor then significant numbers of false positives
may be unnecessarily isolated while false negatives would result in infectious cases remaining
in the community to transmit infection (289). Virus mutations and population immunity may
also undermine the effectiveness of mass testing. Currently most RATs only differentiate
between influenza A and B. Therefore, if both seasonal and pandemic influenza A strains were
co-circulating, as was the case in the early phase of the 2009 H1N1 pandemic, it would not
be possible to determine if the positive cases had seasonal or pandemic influenza, and this
would require PCR confirmation. This will be an important consideration when mass testing for
pandemic influenza using RATs.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 75 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 74 -->

56
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Resource implications

Mass testing can incur considerable direct costs, particularly when carrying out numerous
rounds of the whole population. This could be made less resource intensive depending on the
test that is used and its administration. One proposed method from the SARS-CoV-2 evidence is
pooling PCR tests and only testing the individual samples if a pool is positive. A study carried out
in the USA estimated that pooling up to 24 samples for RT-PCR did not decrease the sensitivity of
tests and decreased the associated costs. It was estimated that individual nasopharyngeal swab
PCR testing would cost a school with a student population of 500 approximately US$ 63 500
per week (US$ 254 000 per month) compared to US$ 11 250 per week (US$ 45 000 per month)
for saliva pooled testing (285). However, high incidences of SARS-CoV-2 infections would yield
a larger number of positive pools, subsequently increasing the number of follow-up PCR tests
required. This in turn would reduce the benefit of the pooling strategy and limit its applicability
in high-incidence settings. Whether tests are distributed to the population for self-testing or are
carried out by health-care personnel will also have its own resource implications. In addition,
if policies require positive RAT results to be confirmed using PCR, then cost-effectiveness may
decrease, unless the prevalence in the community is low.

### Equity and human rights

Mass testing may reveal a concentration of infections among subgroups of the population,
such as those with lower socioeconomic status and/or high-risk workplaces. In such contexts,
care must be taken to avoid discrimination and stigmatization (290). Protections (such as safe
reporting schemes) may also be needed for irregular migrants, including their children, and
others with unclear legal status. Where resources are low, consideration may need to be given to
which groups to prioritize for testing, taking into account equity, non-discrimination and equality
aspects. Mass testing in schools can positively impact on human rights by keeping schools open,
thereby allowing children their right to an education. RATs can provide quick results at lower cost
than PCR, making them more accessible if testing is funded out of pocket.

### Acceptability

Mass testing may not be readily accepted if it is very frequent and/or if the cost is borne by the
individual. In one study, acceptability and adherence to a daily at-home mobile app approach
was assessed among employees of a media company in the USA (291). Participants were mailed
RATs to self-administer at home each day for 21 days, after which they were asked to complete
a quantitative survey and qualitative in-depth interview. Among 31 study participants, the
acceptability of this approach was high, with 71% reporting a preference for it over weekly PCR
testing. Mean adherence to the 21-day testing strategy was also high at 88% (291). Additionally,
in schools that implemented saliva-based pooled PCR testing, teachers reported that students
found the programme acceptable (292). However, in settings where the prevalence of infection is
low, the rate of false positives will be higher, which may reduce the acceptability of such testing
and isolation approaches.

### Feasibility

Increasing testing capacity to efficiently implement mass testing may be challenging in countries
with lower resources, especially if the testing approach of choice requires laboratory testing.
Pooling samples or distributing RATs for self-testing may be more feasible alternatives. However,
although RATs are simple to complete, users would still require guidance on testing frequencies,

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 76 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 75 -->

57
| 6. Active case-finding and contact identification measures
proper testing procedure, interpretation of test results and the appropriate actions to be taken
after a result is generated.

#### 6.3.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Research on the impact of mass testing in terms of reducing influenza virus transmission in
the general population is completely lacking and is required.
Studies of the feasibility and cost-effectiveness of mass testing for influenza could provide
evidence on whether this measure might be effective (and cost-effective) in reducing the
burden of influenza during epidemics and pandemics.

#### 6.3.3 Implementation considerations

•
Mass testing can be considered as an approach for reducing the community transmission
of influenza viruses during periods of high prevalence if a low-cost test with reasonably
high sensitivity and specificity is available.
•
Implementation should only be considered if there are sufficient resources and capacities to
test at large scale, and to handle positive individuals and their close contacts.
•
Where disease prevalence is low, mass testing may be more beneficially targeted to
specific subpopulations or settings.
•
The effectiveness of mass testing alone is small and requires the implementation of other
measures such as isolation of positive individuals and potential quarantining of their close
contacts.
•
If prevention or suppression is the objective, a highly sensitive test would be required and
the cost-benefit ratio may be extremely high.

These studies indicate that the use of a combination of PHSM, including quarantine, was effective
in reducing the public health impact of SARS-CoV-2. One strategy that can complement the
implementation of quarantine orders or recommendations during a pandemic is the use of RATs to
reduce the length of time that individuals need to quarantine. One randomized trial in the United
Kingdom compared the use of daily RATs against the standard 10-day isolation of COVID-19
contacts, and found that daily testing (with removal of self-isolation 24 hours after testing negative)
was non-inferior to standard self-isolation in reducing secondary attack rates (305).

#### 6.4.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The GDG decision for conditional recommendation for voluntary self-quarantine was supported
by very low certainty of evidence and the probable positive balance between desirable and
undesirable health effects of the intervention. The GDG debated the implications on equity
and human rights, values and preference and acceptability and recognized that these may
vary. They noted that compulsory instead of voluntary quarantine carries risks of genderbased violence and violence against children within the home, concerns around freedom of
movement and knock-on effects on other determinants of health and other human rights. The
GDG discussed the resource implications and found to be variable. The GDG believed that the
feasibility of quarantining exposed individuals may vary across settings depending on available
resources, space and facilities.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence that the quarantining of exposed individuals
has an effect on reducing the transmission of influenza or SARS-CoV-2. For influenza, three
observational studies, one modelling study and one quasi-RCT estimated the effectiveness
of quarantine on influenza virus transmission. Of these, four suggested a beneficial effect in
combination with isolation and physical distancing in the community. The remaining study found
an increased risk for contacts quarantining in households. For SARS-CoV-2, 10 observational
studies indicated a beneficial effect of quarantine in terms of delaying the peak of an epidemic,
with some studies suggesting a significant improvement in the effectiveness of quarantine when
combined with other interventions such as case isolation, antiviral prophylaxis or school closure.

### Values and preferences

Values and preferences among quarantined populations are uncertain and variable. One
survey conducted in Türkiye indicated that 69.4% of students believed that quarantine was an
effective intervention in reducing the transmission of influenza (306). In other studies, serious
concerns were expressed by the public regarding potentially harmful outcomes of mandatory
quarantine, such as overcrowding, exposure to infection, and inability to work, shop or contact
family members (307, 308). In another study, fear and a sense of shame were also cited as
concerns by a proportion of the community, with many also believing it to be impolite to maintain
a distance from a sick acquaintance or relative (309). Health-care workers have also reported
fears of acquiring infection during quarantine measures (241) though 86.9% of such respondents
in another study had positive attitudes towards its effectiveness (310).

### Balance of benefits and harms

Although the overall effectiveness of quarantine in reducing the burden of disease and delaying
the peak of an epidemic is likely to be moderate, quarantine may be useful when antiviral drug

individuals with a better understanding of quarantine (OR = 2.27) (316). As with the isolation of
sick individuals, the family structure or infection status of family members affects an individual’s
decision on whether or not to accept quarantine plans (202). Compliance could also decrease as
the length of quarantine increases, especially alongside fears of losing employment.

### Feasibility

There are several barriers and obstacles to the successful implementation of quarantine
of exposed individuals. Home quarantine with infected cases can significantly increase the
risk of acquiring infection (243) while centralized quarantine requires more resources. In
addition, because the incubation period of a pandemic influenza virus strain may initially be
uncertain, home quarantine may at times need to be implemented for an extended period,
leading to financial burdens on families due to work absenteeism (317). Although the use of
quarantine measures appears in 61% of national pandemic plans, detailed strategies for their
implementation are typically not provided and existing infrastructures will vary by country (157).
Depending on the severity of the epidemic or pandemic, the costs of implementation may
outweigh the benefits of quarantine.

#### 6.4.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Most currently available epidemiological studies on the effectiveness of quarantine in
influenza control did not rely completely on laboratory-confirmed influenza as the outcome
of interest, which would be valuable.
•
Although it is difficult to study quarantine using RCTs, robust data from experimental studies
would also be valuable.
•
In addition, more robust evidence is required to understand the contribution and role
of asymptomatic individuals among all infections, the possibility that a “superspreading
event” would occur resulting in a higher number of exposed individuals than usual, and the
determinants of compliance behaviour (202, 318).
•
More evidence on the optimum duration of quarantine is needed.

#### 6.4.3 Implementation considerations

•
Quarantining exposed individuals may not be feasible in some settings depending on
available resources, space and facilities.
•
With the availability of RATs of sufficiently high sensitivity and specificity, the daily testing of
contacts could be considered in lieu of quarantine measures.
•
The duration of quarantine should be informed by the incubation period of the virus.
•
Mandatory quarantine at home or in a facility may be considered in exceptional
circumstances to achieve suppression or mitigation of virus transmission but may have
severe social and economic implications.
•
The costs of quarantine to households and wider society can be high. This measure
could potentially lead to high levels of absenteeism which could negatively impact
business continuity and the provision of essential services during high levels of community
transmission. Governments should consider implementing social security policies to mitigate
the impact of this measure, especially in terms of addressing wealth inequalities and risk to
vulnerable groups such as women and children.

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

## 7. Social measures


### 7.1 Indoor hospitality and recreation


## measures


### Conditional recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

WHO suggests against risk-mitigation measures in indoor hospitality and recreational settings to
reduce the transmission of influenza viruses during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Remarks

•
Indoor hospitality and recreation venues (including restaurants, bars, cinemas, gyms
and beauty salons) were identified as locations associated with increased risk of virus
transmission during the COVID-19 pandemic (319). As policies on face coverings (including
face masks) in public places were implemented in many jurisdictions during the COVID-19
pandemic (320, 321), these venues represented locations in which “mask-off” activities such
as eating, drinking and exercising still occurred outside individual residences.

### Summary of evidence

The systematic review did not identify any published evidence on the effectiveness of indoor
hospitality and recreation measures in reducing influenza transmission.

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

Nine studies (five epidemiological studies (167, 322–325) and four simulation studies (326–329)
were identified that investigated the effects of measures implemented in catering and fitness
premises. Most of the measures assessed aimed to reduce SARS-CoV-2 transmission by
improving indoor air quality and/or limiting the consumption of food and drink on the premises
(for example, by limiting business hours, restricting alcohol consumption and encouraging
outdoor dining). The effectiveness of such measures in reducing SARS-CoV-2 transmission
varied. In China, Hong Kong SAR one study observed reduced secondary attack rates in
restaurants after new legislation mandated improved ventilation or installation of air purifiers in
dine-in catering premises (167), while another study did not identify any statistically significant
changes in Rt when reduced business hours for catering services were implemented (324).

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### Resource implications

Hospitality and recreation industries make up a significant proportion of many economies.
Disruptions to their operations will likely incur significant costs not only to business owners but
also to employees and the wider economy in terms of GDP loss. Privately owned small and
medium businesses in these sectors are particularly vulnerable to disruption. Statistics Canada
reported a 55.8% drop in employment in the food services and drinking places subsector by May

## 2020 compared to pre-pandemic levels in February 2020, and this only recovered to around

a 30% drop by December 2020 (331). The real GDP contribution of food services and drinking
places in Canada fell by 39.5% in March 2020 and by another 40.8% in April 2020 following
permanent closures, and with surviving businesses operating at greatly reduced capacity (331).

### Equity and human rights

The need to prevent the health-care system from becoming overwhelmed needs to be balanced
against the potential loss of employment in the absence of any financial support measure
for employees and business owners in the hospitality industry, many of whom depend on the
industry for their livelihoods (332). Hospitality services in particular can also represent food
security and social connection for selected subpopulations, which are crucial for individual
physical and mental health.

### Acceptability

The acceptability of hospitality and recreation measures among the public may depend on
the type of measures undertaken and their duration. Efforts to improve indoor air quality are
likely to be accepted as it is the least disruptive to consumers, even though there may be cost
implications for business owners.

### Feasibility

It is currently not known if hospitality and recreation measures alone would have a large effect
in reducing influenza transmission. However, such measures were implemented at large scale,
along with other PHSM, in many countries during the COVID-19 pandemic.
7.1.2
Knowledge gaps
•
Evidence of the effectiveness of measures that could be implemented in indoor hospitality
and recreational facilities (including restaurants, bars, cinemas, gyms and beauty salons)
on influenza transmission is needed. However, large-scale RCTs are unlikely to be feasible.
•
The effectiveness of some of the measures applied during the COVID-19 pandemic (such
as limiting business hours and venue capacity) could usefully be investigated for potential
application in reducing influenza transmission during influenza epidemics and pandemics.
•
Improved understanding of the dynamics of human-to-human influenza virus transmission
would be beneficial.
7.1.3
Implementation considerations
•
In indoor settings where mask-off activities can occur, measures to reduce the risk of
influenza transmission (for example, restricting business hours, setting maximum capacities,
requiring proof of a negative test and/or vaccination, mask wearing where possible and

7.2.1

### Conditional recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

WHO suggests considering restrictions on gatherings to reduce the transmission of influenza
Influenza viruses are transmitted between individuals through the air (that is, through
inhalation or the direct deposition of infectious respiratory particles on the facial mucosa) and
to a lesser extent through indirect contact (that is, through fomites) (333). As mass gatherings
and crowded areas tend to increase person-to-person close contact, measures that reduce
such contact could potentially reduce the community transmission of influenza (333).
A risk-based approach based on the three steps of risk evaluation, risk mitigation and risk
communication to inform the decision to restrict, modify, postpone, cancel or proceed with
the holding of any gathering should be applied in the decision-making process.
Three observational studies and one modelling study examining the effects of restricting
gatherings were identified in the review (55, 334–336). One study of World Youth Day 2008
with sleeping in one large hall (335). Another two studies based on the 1918 Spanish influenza
pandemic found that timely bans on public gatherings and the closure of public places

### Equity and human rights

Restrictions on gatherings should serve a strictly necessary public health purpose, be
implemented in accordance with the law and principles of non-discrimination, and be subject
to regular review to minimize interference with the rights to freedom of peaceful assembly,
association, religion and expression, and to avoid any interference with democratic electoral
processes (365). Where restrictions on gatherings are introduced, rights to information, freedom
of expression and association online should be guaranteed (367). Gatherings can be a source
of information during influenza pandemics, which can comfort people and reduce fear. The
cancelling of religious gatherings also has implications for the right to practise one’s religion.
While it may not be possible to cancel some events (for example, the Hajj), such measures can
be implemented when the impact of disease transmission greatly outweighs the social impact of
cancellations. During the first year of the COVID-19 pandemic, the government of Saudi Arabia
announced very stringent restrictions on the total number of pilgrims attending the Hajj, with
zero COVID-19-positive tests subsequently reported among those allowed to attend (368).

### Acceptability

Public acceptance of restrictions on gatherings will likely depend on the type and importance of
the gathering (243). In one survey conducted in Australia in 2007, 94.2% of respondents reported
being willing to avoid public events (314), while a polling study in five countries (Argentina, Japan,
Mexico, United Kingdom and the USA) in 2010 found that 11–69% of respondents would prefer
to avoid places where many people gather (for example, shopping centres or sports venues)
during a pandemic (369). However, some people might oppose the mandatory cancellation of
religious gatherings during a pandemic (365). During a WHO consultation on the response to
influenza A(H1N1)pdm09, most reporting countries stated that they had not instituted restrictions
on gatherings and were taking a wait-and-see approach for any upcoming events (370).

### Feasibility

These measures are likely to be feasible in the community. However, not all settings may have
the infrastructural capacity to physically separate event attendees by a specified distance.

#### 7.2.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Although restricting gatherings and increasing physical distancing may be effective in
reducing influenza transmission, it remains difficult to disaggregate their independent
effects from those of other PHSM implemented in parallel.
•
Significant gaps remain in the understanding of human-to-human transmission dynamics
and related behavioural insights.
•
Studies are needed to assess the role of gatherings in influenza transmission in the community.

#### 7.2.3 Implementation considerations

•
Decision-making process for holding, modifying, postponing or cancelling gatherings during
influenza epidemics and pandemics should be risk-based, with an emphasis placed on risk
assessment, risk mitigation and risk communication. Risk assessment should involve all relevant
stakeholders, including national and local health authorities, as well as event organizers.
•
In the decision-making process, aspects such as indoor versus outdoor gatherings, types of
activity (for example, singing), timing of the intervention (early in the pandemic versus late)
and prevalence of the infection should be taken into account.

There is a risk that very stringent restrictions could push gatherings underground, as was seen

### 7.3 School measures and closures


### Conditional recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

To reduce the transmission of influenza viruses in the community, WHO suggests that:
•
the implementation of one or more school measures be considered as sustainable
alternatives to full school closure during influenza epidemics and pandemics; and
•
coordinated proactive school closure be considered during influenza epidemics and
pandemics of high or extraordinary severity.

### Remarks

•
School-age children are particularly important in influenza virus transmission in the
community, with attack rates typically highest in this age group during epidemics
and pandemics.
•
School measures to reduce influenza transmission vary from relatively straightforward
interventions (such as increasing the distance between desks) to far more drastic and
stringent interventions (such as completely closing schools). Examples of school measures
include exclusion policies for ill children, reducing mixing between classes, staggering of
recesses and/or lunch breaks, mask wearing, frequent testing, and closure of individual
classes or schools triggered by a defined number of cases.
•
In principle, the above recommendation refers to all levels of education, from early
childhood care and education centres to primary and secondary schools. However, the
effect of school measures and closures in reducing the transmission of different influenza
subtypes and other respiratory viruses may vary according to the impact of the disease on
school-age children and their role in disease transmission.

### Summary of evidence

No studies examining the effect of school measures on influenza virus transmission were
identified during the current systematic review. For school closures, a previous systematic review
of 79 epidemiological studies had suggested that this intervention could reduce the transmission
of seasonal and pandemic influenza among schoolchildren. However, due to the heterogeneity
of the data, the optimum strategy (such as length of closure, and whether it should be reactive
or proactive) remained unclear (60). The current systematic review updated the previous review
by identifying 126 additional epidemiological studies that met the inclusion criteria, giving a total
evidence base of 205 studies, of which 116 had examined the effectiveness of school closures on
influenza virus transmission, 85 on SARS-CoV-2 transmission and four on multiple respiratory
virus transmission.
The effectiveness of proactive school closures in reducing influenza virus transmission in schools
and the community was estimated in 26 studies. For example, one study investigated the
effect of school closures in Switzerland during the 1918 Spanish influenza pandemic and noted
reduced epidemic growth in those regions that had implemented this measure (371), while
two recent studies in Hong Kong SAR, China observed reduced cumulative incidence (59) and
reduced Rt of influenza in the community (59, 372). Other studies conducted during the COVID-19
pandemic found that school closures implemented during the first year of the pandemic were

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
epidemic size, incidence rates, and subsequent morbidity and mortality, while most of the
other studies (n = 28) did not find a statistically significant association between school closure
and SARS-CoV-2 transmission, or did not observe any changes in the trajectory of SARS-CoV-2
transmission in the community (413). One study found a positive association between school
closure and infection rates (337) while another found that school closures were only effective
when stringent contact tracing was also implemented (218). As school measures and school
closures are rarely implemented in the absence of other PHSM, their effectiveness in reducing
the transmission of respiratory viruses cannot always be disaggregated.
7.3.1
Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members
The GDG decision for conditional recommendation for school measures and closures was
supported by very low certainty evidence and the probable positive balance between desirable
and undesirable health effects of the intervention. GDG agreed that the effect of school
measures and closures in reducing the transmission of different influenza subtypes and other
respiratory viruses may vary according to the impact of the disease on school-age children and
their role in disease transmission. The members debated the implications on equity and human
rights and valued as uncertain since the right to education would need to be balance against the
right to health. However, the members recognized that school closures raise major human rights
issues for children, their families and communities and may exacerbate existing inequalities.
Members agreed that resource implications, values and preference and acceptability as well
as the feasibility may vary. The GDG thought that the implementation of one or more school
measures be sustainable alternatives to full school closure to minimize the impact on child’s
education, development and well-being.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence with regard to school measures and closures, with
published studies reporting or predicting small to moderate effects on reducing influenza virus
transmission. Proactive closures and planned school holidays were associated with a moderate
impact on influenza transmission, while the observed impact of reactive school closures was
generally limited. Improved effectiveness of school closure was seen when the measure was
combined with other interventions.

### Values and preferences

School closures and class dismissals due to outbreaks of infectious disease have been
implemented in many locations before the COVID-19 pandemic as important measures in
controlling diseases common in children, such as hand, foot, and mouth disease, measles, and
less commonly respiratory virus infections such as influenza (414). Little variability was observed
in the importance that populations assign to school closures – for example, in one survey in
the USA, 92% of caregivers and 89% of teachers reported the belief that school closures were
somewhat effective in reducing influenza cases among school-age children (414). However, the
prolonged school closures and class dismissals implemented during the COVID-19 pandemic
have influenced parental acceptance and preferences with regard to such measures as they had
to balance their child’s education, development and well-being against their need to work and
provide for their family (415).
