their main source of income. The members debated the implications on values and preference
and acceptability and recognized that these may vary and would depend on several factors,
including the nature of the intervention, its stringency, and the impact on daily life. The members
judged the feasibility of the intervention as variable; they noted that while stay-at-home
provision could be feasible in certain settings it is likely to be highly resource intensive. The
low certainty evidence together with these considerations caused the GDG to conclude with a
conditional recommendation against stay-at-home provisions.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is low certainty evidence on whether stay-at-home provisions can reduce the
transmission of influenza viruses. During the COVID-19 pandemic, the majority of studies
reported reductions in SARS-CoV-2 transmission following such provisions.

### Values and preferences

There is uncertainty and variability in the importance that populations assign to stay-at-home
provisions to reduce influenza transmission. Perceptions of the expected outcomes of stay-athome provisions differ between different groups. One study of Australian survey data on public
attitudes towards health initiatives during the COVID-19 pandemic revealed that around half
of all respondents were equally concerned about the health impacts of the pandemic and its
economic impact (470). The trade-offs between protecting public health and disruptions to daily
and economic activities are often most acutely experienced by disadvantaged subpopulations in
both developed and developing economies (471, 472).

### Balance of benefits and harms

Stay-at-home provisions, in combination with other social measures, have the potential to
reduce influenza transmission in the community, but there is limited evidence to determine the
individual effects of such provisions. Severe restriction of mobility can impact individual physical
and mental health (473, 474), and affect incomes and access to basic needs, including food,
safety, shelter and health care, especially among disadvantaged groups (475–477). Therefore,
both the short- and long-term impacts of these extreme measures on society’s most vulnerable
groups need to be balanced against the impacts of a pandemic on the entire population and its
health-care system. As such, stay-at-home provisions will likely be justifiable only during severe
pandemics with high mortality rates and global spread. Judicious consideration of the triggers
and planned relaxation of such measures, accompanied by steps to protect disadvantaged
populations, is warranted.

### Resource implications

Although the direct costs of implementing and enforcing stay-at-home provisions may be
minimal, there will be significant resource implications in terms of revenue and income lost by
businesses and individuals who rely on on-site work as their main source of income. With regard
to the COVID-19 pandemic, the European Central Bank noted that although the shock of the early

### 7.5 Workplace measures and closures


### Conditional recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

To reduce the transmission of influenza viruses in community, WHO suggests that:
•
remote working from home be encouraged where feasible during influenza epidemics
and pandemics; and
•
proactive workplace closures be considered during influenza epidemics and pandemics
of high or extraordinary severity.

### Remarks

•
Although workplaces can be the foci of influenza outbreaks among the working-age
population, the associated levels of morbidity and mortality are relatively low in this
population as they tend to experience less severe symptoms compared with school-age
children and older adults. However, such outbreaks increase the risks of secondary
transmission to family members under their care, many of whom will be school age
or elderly.

### Summary of evidence

The current systematic review identified three epidemiological studies (295, 480, 481) included
in an earlier systematic review (482) of the effectiveness of workplace physical distancing
measures as well as three additional studies (483–485). The three epidemiological studies had
suggested that physical distancing in the workplace was associated with reductions in ILI or
seroconversion to 2009 influenza A(H1N1). In addition, one quasi-cluster RCT in Japan showed
that paid sick-leave policies in the workplace reduced the overall risk of influenza A (H1N1) by
about 20% in one influenza season (295), with two further epidemiological studies conducted in
the USA indicating that the provision of paid sick leave could help to decrease influenza-related
absenteeism, thereby reducing transmission rates (480, 481). Workplace measures combined
with other interventions (such as school closures, personal protection measures and antiviral
drug use) showed greater effectiveness (245).
The current review also identified one ecological study into the role of PHSM including workplace
closures in nine tropical Asian countries (486). In this study, seasonal influenza epidemics
were either not observed or delayed following PHSM implementation. However, the authors
did not find statistically significant associations between workplace closures and reduced
influenza transmission.

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

Of 26 studies, identified in the systematic review, that investigated the association between
workplace closures and SARS-CoV-2 transmission (including closure of non-essential business
sectors) (216, 218, 221, 339, 340, 347, 349, 350, 357, 359, 362, 440, 452, 453, 466, 487-497),

## 23 reported small to moderate effectiveness in reducing community transmission. The

effectiveness of workplace closures in reducing COVID-19 mortality varied, with some studies
identifying small to moderate effects while others identified no association between workplace
closures and COVID-19 deaths.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 100 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 99 -->

81
| 7. Social measures
As workplace measures were often implemented in parallel with other PHSM, their effects are
generally difficult to disaggregate. However, there is a consensus that other PHSM will enhance
the effectiveness of workplace measures when implemented in concert.
7.5.1
Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members
The GDG decision for conditional recommendation for workplace measures and closures was
supported by very low certainty evidence and the probable positive balance between desirable
and undesirable health effects of the intervention. The members judged workplace measures
and closures to probably be in accordance with universal human rights, though the right to work
would need to be balanced against the right to health. Workplace measures and closures may,
however, exacerbate socioeconomic inequities since loss of income can lead to impoverishment
and limited ability to pay for necessities. The members debated the resource implications, and
they judged that workplace measures and closures might have significant adverse economic
impacts on individuals and governments. Members recognized that values and preference
and acceptability as well as the feasibility may vary. Overall, the GDG believed that mandated
workplace closure is unlikely to be feasible in the long term. The GDG thought that work
measures be sustainable alternatives to full work closure to minimize the impact on adverse
effects on individuals, communities, the workforce and the economy. The GDG highlighted the
need for policy-makers to implement social protection policies to mitigate the potential adverse
effect of work measures.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence that workplace measures and closures can reduce
influenza transmission. Five observational studies and one RCT indicated that workplace
measures such as working from home and paid sick leave can reduce influenza transmission in
the workplace. One observational study reported no significant benefit of workplace closures
in reducing influenza transmission. Eight of nine observational studies that had estimated the
effectiveness of workplace measures in reducing SARS-CoV-2 transmission found beneficial
effects. Of the 26 observational studies that had estimated the effectiveness of workplace
closures in reducing SARS-CoV-2 transmission, 23 reported benefits when such closures were
implemented in combination with other PHSM.

### Values and preferences

There is both uncertainty and variability in the reported importance that populations assign
to workplace measures to reduce influenza transmission, with responses varying among
subpopulations with different employment and socioeconomic circumstances. One study in the
Netherlands (Kingdom of the) reported that 30% of respondents believed that staying home
from work is an efficacious means of reducing influenza transmission (364). However, in another
study, 93% of New York State, USA residents believed that staying home is effective in preventing
influenza transmission (498). Another study in the USA showed that 28% of employed respondents
reported that they might lose their jobs or businesses as a result of having to stay home from
work for 7–10 days in the event of an influenza pandemic (246). This would also cause severe
personal economic difficulties among some members of the community – though less so for
those who received pay while they worked remotely (246).
Only limited studies have been conducted into the values and perceptions among the
population regarding the potential consequences of workplace closures. One study indicated

83
| 7. Social measures
or for jobs that require hands-on work. Employees may be more willing to accept workplace
closures if there is no anxiety regarding their job security and income (250). It is considered likely
that employers and national authorities will tend to resist workplace closures due to their high
operational costs.

### Feasibility

Remote work, paid-leave policies and staggered-shift measures are unlikely to be feasible in
many circumstances. Workplace closures are also likely to raise significant feasibility issues,
for example where companies provide essential services to the community or facilitate off-site
working, and thus cannot be closed. Overall, the GDG believed that mandated workplace
closure is unlikely to be feasible in the long term. However, it might be considered in the short to
medium term to mitigate the impacts of community transmission during a pandemic. Planning
for such a scenario could allow businesses and financial markets to anticipate and prepare for
potential disruptions and mitigation measures.

#### 7.5.2 Knowledge gaps

•
The systematic review identified some evidence on the effectiveness of workplace
measures and closures on influenza transmission. However, measures such as providing
segregated working areas for people with mild symptoms have not been studied in depth.
More research on the role of the workplace in the transmission of epidemic or pandemic
influenza viruses is needed.
•
More research is needed on the optimal triggers, timing and duration of workplace closures
in order to maximize the impact of this highly disruptive intervention.
•
There is a need for a comprehensive review of the ethical issues associated with workplace
measures, and for comparisons of the benefits and costs of implementing these measures.
•
Studies are also needed on the feasibility of implementation of workplace measures, and
the potential socioeconomic impacts on individuals and communities.

#### 7.5.3 Implementation considerations

•
When remote working is not feasible, other workplace measures (such as staggering
shifts and/or mask wearing) or reactive closures can be considered, with gradation of
interventions based on pandemic severity.
•
In certain circumstances, workplace measures and closures may exacerbate socioeconomic
inequities since loss of income can lead to impoverishment and limited ability to pay for
necessities. Consideration should be given to mitigating these and other adverse effects on
individuals, communities, the workforce and the economy through the implementation of
social policies.
•
When considering measures and closures, workplaces might usefully be sub-grouped – for
example, into those that may not need to remain open for society to function and those that
may have to remain open (such as health care facilities and food shops).

## 8. Travel-related measures


### 8.1 Entry and exit screening at points


## of entry


### Strong recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

WHO recommends against syndromic entry and exit screening at points of entry to reduce the
transmission of influenza viruses in the community during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Remarks

•
In this guideline, entry and exit screening at points of entry specifically refers to syndromic
screening for influenza using one or more methods, such as temperature check,
observation and health declaration forms. Although test-based screening methods were
also reviewed and discussed by the GDG, the above recommendation only relates to
syndromic screening.
•
Fever and other symptom screening is not sufficiently sensitive (meaning that infected
people will enter the community), and also has poor specificity (meaning that symptomatic
individuals not infected with influenza may overwhelm the health system).

### Summary of evidence

Four observational studies, two modelling studies and one simulation study included in this
review assessed the effectiveness of syndromic entry and exit screening in reducing influenza
transmission (398, 504–509). Observational studies conducted at two airports in New Zealand
and one in Japan estimated that the sensitivity of entry screening was low (504–506). Another
observational study conducted in China during the 2009 H1N1 influenza pandemic found that
among arriving international travellers, half of all influenza cases were identified more than
a day after arrival (through passive case finding and contact tracing in the community) even
though 37% of these cases had been screened while passing through the border entry site (air,
sea or land) (398). The two modelling studies estimated that entry screening of international
travelers may help to delay local transmission by 1–2 weeks (507, 508). No published studies on
the effect of exit screening on influenza transmission were identified.

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The review identified six studies that assessed the effectiveness of entry and/or exit screening
in reducing SARS-CoV-2 transmission (510–515). One observational study assessing symptom

<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 103 -->

exit screening at points of entry.

There was no evidence found for exit screening.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 105 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 104 -->

86
| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza
instantaneous and non-invasive nature. One study conducted in Japan during the 2009 H1N1
influenza pandemic reported that the sensitivity of infrared thermometers was 50.8–70.4% and
their specificity 63.6–81.7% (517). Another study conducted in New Zealand reported that the
sensitivity of infrared thermal image scanners was 84–86% and their specificity 31–71% (518). It is
possible that some travellers with fever might opt to take antipyretics to reduce their symptoms
before travel and thus avoid detection by thermal scanners or thermometers.

### Balance of benefits and harms

No literature on the harm of syndromic screening of travellers was identified. As influenza cases
may remain asymptomatic for several days (up to 2 days for seasonal influenza) (398), symptom
presentation varies and screening methods are imperfect (519), traveller screening for symptoms
of influenza virus infection is associated with major limitations in preventing the introduction of
influenza into a location or reducing the overall attack rate and duration of an epidemic (506).
The GDG considered the asymptomatic period of influenza infections and the low sensitivity and
specificity of screening methods, and agreed that the effectiveness of syndromic screening of
travellers is likely to be very limited for influenza.

### Resource implications

Substantial public health resources would be required, including adequate numbers of trained
staff, screening devices and adequate infrastructure to conduct effective traveller screening
(506). These resources are often diverted from other critical response actions such as outbreak
investigation. Follow-up actions for those screening positive can also incur costs in terms of
laboratory resources for confirmation and further treatment. In addition, where syndromic
screening methods have poor specificity, the number of uninfected individuals wrongly screening
positive could overwhelm the health system.

### Equity and human rights

All screening needs to be carefully designed and implemented to respect the dignity and privacy
of travellers (520). To the extent possible, when implementing syndromic screening, authorities
should consider seeking the prior and informed consent of travellers. Where informed consent is
not possible or advisable, then information on what data will be used, how and by whom should
be provided.

### Acceptability

Infrared thermometers continue to be used in some points of entry to screen travellers, and is
generally accepted by policy-makers as a “visible” public health measure. Exit screening was
not implemented in the 2009 H1N1 influenza pandemic, and its acceptability in preventing or
delaying the introduction of influenza at the destination site is uncertain.

### Feasibility

Syndromic entry screening is used in some points of entry and has been shown to be feasible.

<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 105 -->

87
| 8. Travel-related measures

#### 8.1.2 Knowledge gaps

•
There are no high-quality studies on the effectiveness of entry screening in preventing the
importation of influenza infections and reducing influenza transmission, and no evidence
at all on exit screening. Quantitative studies evalulating the implementation of screening
programmes at different times, in different combinations and for different pathogens
are required to estimate the potential value of screening in preventing the importation or
exportation of cases and the seeding of epidemics in the population.

#### 8.1.3 Implementation considerations

•
Some locations routinely monitor the temperature of incoming travellers in an effort to
identify those with symptoms of Ebola disease, Middle East respiratory syndrome or
other emerging infectious disease. The above recommendation only applies to influenza
epidemics and pandemics.
•
Whenever entry and exit screening is implemented, policy-makers should consider
allocating additional resources for the follow-up of detected cases or their referral to
health-care services or quarantine arrangements as needed.
•
To improve screening outcomes, some countries may consider using rapid tests before exit or
at arrival – however, high test sensitivity and specificity would be an important requirement.
Moreover, rapid tests may not pick up individuals during the incubation period.

<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 106 -->

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

### 8.2 Travel advice


### Strong recommendation for – very low certainty evidence

WHO recommends that travel advice be used to inform individuals of the risk of infection and
of the measures that can be taken to protect themselves against potential exposure to influenza
viruses during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Summary of evidence

The review identified one study that measured the effect of travel advice on the impact of
seasonal influenza across nine countries in Asia (486). This study estimated that for each step
increase from advice only to restrictions on internal movements (that is, from no measures, to
recommending not to travel domestically in limited geographical areas, to recommending not to
travel domestically nationally, to restrictions on internal movements in limited geographical areas
and to restrictions on internal movements nationally), the average proportion of respiratory
specimens that were positive for influenza virus decreased by 41% (relative proportion ratio =
0.59; 95% CI = 0.36–0.96).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The review identified one simulation study conducted during the COVID-19 pandemic in

## 17 countries and 23 states in the USA. It was estimated that a recommendation not to travel

between cities/regions reduced the weekly growth rate of COVID-19 by around 26% (361).

#### 8.2.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The GDG decision for strong recommendation for travel advice was supported by very
low certainty evidence and the balance between health benefits and harms favoring the
intervention. The members judged travel advice to be in accordance with universal human right
for health and information. The GDG discussed the resource implications and valued as variable;
they noted that an economic impact may be associated to the intervention but likely to be
cost-effective. The members agreed that travel advice would probably be acceptable for the key
stakeholders, including general public, local authorities, governments, national and international
travel industry. The GDG considered providing travel advice to be feasible given the absence of
any reported feasibility issues to date.

### Certainty of the evidence

Overall, there is very low certainty evidence for the effectiveness of travel advice in reducing
the impact of influenza or SARS-CoV-2. It is difficult to separate out the role or contribution of
travel advice from those of other PHSM implemented at the same time. However, it remains a
key recommendation in influenza epidemics and pandemics due to its effectiveness in making
people aware of health risks and potentially protective behaviours. There is a lack of evidence on
the optimal timing of travel advice in order to achieve the greatest impact.

| 8. Travel-related measures

### 8.3 Travel restrictions


### Conditional recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

WHO suggests against travel restrictions (including border closures) to reduce the transmission
of influenza viruses in the community during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Remarks

•
There is weak evidence, mainly from modelling studies, that points of entry closures may
only delay the introduction of infections for a short period, while adversely affecting
mitigation programmes and disrupting supply chains. They may also be unacceptable to
individuals and communities who may rely on domestic or international mobility for their
livelihoods, and for access to essential goods and services. Based on experiences during
the COVID-19 pandemic, border controls in sea-locked locations (such as island nations)
could be effective in temporarily preventing the importation of cases if such controls are
implemented before seeding events in the community. Therefore, such locations may
consider border closures during severe pandemics but must weigh the potential impact of a
local epidemic against the potentially serious social and economic consequences.

### Summary of evidence

Two studies (336, 523) related to the impact of international travel restrictions (including border
closures) on influenza epidemics and pandemics were identified. One time-series analysis of
ecological data conducted in the USA indicated that the frequency of domestic airline travel
was temporally associated with the rate of influenza transmission, and a marked reduction in
such travel following the events of 11 September 2001 delayed the epidemic peak by 13 days
compared with the average for other years (523). The second study evaluated the effect of

## 14 PHSM on influenza transmission, including travel restrictions (travel bans and border closure),

using influenza virological surveillance data from 33 countries. In this study, banning arrivals
from selected high-risk regions was not effective in suppressing influenza transmission. However,
complete travel bans or border closure were found to have some effect in suppressing influenza
transmission (336).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The review identified twenty-six studies that examined the effectiveness of travel restrictions,
excluding border closure, on SARS-CoV-2 transmission. Of these studies, four evaluated the
effectiveness of such measures in reducing the importation risk of SARSCoV2 or delaying the
epidemic. One modelling study and one observational study estimated that the implementation
of international or domestic travel restrictions in the early stage of a pandemic would decrease
the risk of case importations in places with low epidemic levels, thereby delaying local
transmission and gaining time to prepare measures to respond to the pandemic (524, 525).
However, one observational study conducted in Japan indicated that international travel
restrictions implemented against travel from China in the early stage of the COVID-19 pandemic
only delayed a major epidemic by 2 days (526). Similarly, a modelling study conducted across

## 80 countries suggested that international travel restrictions had only a minimal impact on the risk

of virus importation (527).

mandatory restrictions separate family members with little to no compassionate exemptions in
place. Border closures are likely to be acceptable for short periods of time but not when they are
prolonged, and especially when the perceived severity of infection is low (552).

### Feasibility

Some countries already include travel restriction plans in their national pandemic preparedness
plans, while others cannot implement such plans due to national laws or ill-defined or porous
borders. Border closure in a severe pandemic is technically feasible and, to some extent,
acceptable to many (552) and may be most effective when implemented in the very early stage
of a pandemic. However, travel restrictions (including border closures) may be challenging to
implement because of legal, ethical, economic and resource implications. The GDG considered
that it would likely be challenging to sustain travel restrictions, and to bear their substantial
negative societal and economic costs, over the longer term.

#### 8.3.2 Knowledge gaps

•
No high-quality studies on the effectiveness of travel restrictions, including border closures,
were identified. Studies assessing the effectiveness of travel restrictions and their costeffectiveness would be valuable for informing decisions on their use, and to identify
potential barriers to their implementation.

#### 8.3.3 Implementation considerations

•
Countries (for example, island nations) that can successfully implement travel restrictions
(such as border closures aimed at preventing or delaying importation of the virus) could
consider the use of such measures in the short term. All countries implementing travel
restrictions, including border closure, shall notify WHO as required by the IHR.
•
Whenever implemented, such measures should be periodically evaluated and if necessary
adjusted to minimize potential negative impacts on the economy and society.
•
Whenever such measures are implemented, special consideration should be given to
settings in which implementation and/or adherence is likely to be challenging, such as
during an ongoing humanitarian crisis. Exemptions should also be made for those seeking
medical care abroad or caring for an ill family member, or for family reunifications.

95
| 8. Travel-related measures

### 8.4 Upon-arrival quarantine


### Conditional recommendation against – very low certainty evidence

WHO suggests against the use of upon-arrival quarantine of travellers to delay the introduction
of influenza viruses into the community during influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### Remarks

•
Upon-arrival quarantine that takes place at a point of entry is defined as border
quarantine. Maritime quarantine refers to the monitoring of all passengers and crew for a
defined period before disembarkation from a ship is permitted in a jurisdiction (553), while
airport or onboard quarantine refers to the monitoring of all passengers and crew for a
defined period before disembarkation from a flight is permitted (554).

### Summary of evidence

Four studies were identified in the systematic review, two of which were observational. One
study assessed onboard/airport quarantine and found only minimal impact on detecting and
preventing the entry of cases; however, following up with passengers thereafter was found to
be effective in preventing secondary infection from travellers (554). Maritime quarantine in
small island nations was reported to have delayed or prevented the arrival of the 1918 Spanish
influenza pandemic, indirectly reducing mortality in the region (553). A simulation study in Japan
suggested only minimal impact of quarantine at border on the number of cases entering a
country (555). However, another simulation study suggested that quarantine at border of 9 days
would prevent the entry of 99% of infected travellers into small island nations (318).

### Relevant evidence from the COVID-19 pandemic

The review identified fourteen studies that assessed the impact of upon-arrival quarantine
on the spread of SARSCoV-2 (218, 511, 535, 556–566), four of which were observational studies
(218, 556, 561, 562). One study analyzed data from the Test and Trace system in the United
Kingdom along with genomic surveillance data, and determined that 14-day quarantine was
associated with reduced number of contacts and reduced (but not completely eliminated)
onward transmission of imported cases (556). A simulation study estimated the time to outbreak
in New Zealand (compared to a baseline of 2.4 months) following the implementation of
a 14-day quarantine combined with pre-flight testing, and estimated a median time to an
outbreak of 4.9 years (565). Another modelling study carried out in Ghana assessed the different
periods during which different PHSM were implemented. The period that included mandatory
quarantine for all arrivals (among other PHSM) led to a projected 6% decrease in infection rate –
however, this was not considered to be statistically significant (561).

#### 8.4.1 Justifications and summary of considerations of GDG members

The evidence identified was rated of very low certainty. The GDG judged the balance of health
benefit and harms to probably favor the intervention. The members discussed the resource
implications and valued as variable. They noted that the harm on society and the economy may
be substantial against a small effect in delaying the start of an epidemic overall.

compared to voluntary quarantine (569). In addition, if upon-arrival quarantine is implemented

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 116 -->
<!-- SOURCE_PAGE_INDEX: 115 -->

97
| 8. Travel-related measures
increased risk of virus transmission during the quarantine period (571). Maritime quarantine
and onboard quarantine are subject to similar concerns. Although onboard quarantine involves
a shorter duration of restriction of movement, currently available evidence suggests that the
intervention has low cost-effectiveness and low effectiveness in general for influenza control.
Additionally, consideration will need to be given to exemptions for individuals providing essential
services or for compassionate reasons. This may include truck drivers, seafarers, air crews and
people experiencing family or medical emergencies.

### Acceptability

The reported acceptability of, and compliance with, upon-arrival quarantine is varied. In one
telephone survey conducted in Australia, more than 90% of respondents reported being willing to
stay at home, especially after being given brief information about pandemic influenza (314). Two
other studies reached similar conclusions, with 94% (315) and 92.8% (310) of respondents reporting
adherence to a quarantine recommendation. However, a cross-sectional survey in Australia
found that only 53% of households reported being fully compliant with quarantine. Compliance
was better among individuals with greater understanding of the purpose of quarantine (OR
= 2.27; 95% CI = 1.35–3.80) (316). Adherence may decrease as the duration of the upon-arrival
quarantine period increases.

### Feasibility

There are several barriers to the successful implementation of upon-arrival quarantine of
potentially exposed individuals. In addition, as the incubation period of a novel pandemic
influenza strain may be uncertain, self-quarantining at home may at times need to be
implemented for an extended period, and may thus place financial burdens on families due
to work absenteeism (317). Depending on the severity of the epidemic or pandemic, the cost of
implementing upon-arrival quarantine may outweigh its benefits.

#### 8.4.2 Knowledge gaps

•
Evidence for the effect of upon-arrival quarantine on influenza transmission is limited.
Although it is difficult to study on-arrival quarantine using RCTs, robust data from
experimental studies would be valuable.
•
More evidence on the viral shedding patterns of asymptomatic influenza may inform the
feasibility and optimal duration of upon-arrival quarantine to delay the importation of
influenza cases.

#### 8.4.3 Implementation considerations

•
Countries with adequate resources aiming to delay the introduction of pandemic influenza
cases could consider implementing facility- or home-based upon-arrival quarantine for
all travellers as part of their border measures. However, the successful implementation of
upon-arrival quarantine would require significant resources.
•
If not implemented properly, there may a risk of introducing an additional source of virus
transmission within upon-arrival quarantine facilities.

| WHO guideline on public health and social measures for mitigating the risk and impact of epidemic and pandemic influenza

## 9. Guideline dissemination,


## monitoring and updating


### 9.1 Publication and dissemination of


## the guideline

This guideline is available on the WHO Global Influenza Programme (GIP) website. To support and
facilitate its dissemination to relevant departments in ministries of health, briefing materials will be
developed for technical officers in WHO regional and country offices. Moreover, the guideline will
be disseminated among the members of the Global Influenza Surveillance and Response System
(GISRS) and partner agencies. Originally developed and published in English, the need to translate
the guideline into all six WHO official languages will be assessed based on country needs and in
collaboration with WHO regional offices.
Derivative products
WHO GIP, in coordination with the WHO Steering Group and technical officers in WHO regional
offices will identify and develop suitable derivative products, which may include a policy
brief, infographics and training materials to support countries in the implementation of PHSM
recommendations during future influenza epidemics and pandemics.

### 9.2 Monitoring and evaluating the impact of


## the guideline

WHO will continually collect feedback from guideline implementers and stakeholders to
evaluate its usefulness and impact. This information will be used to evaluate the effects of the
guideline on health outcomes, ensure the quality of the guidance provided and identify areas
for future improvement. In particular, upon implementation of the recommendations during an
influenza epidemic or pandemic, the impact of the guideline will be measured using influenza
transmission-related outcomes such as the number of infections, hospitalizations and deaths.
Ideally, baseline measures will be used to assess performance in relation to the changes
resulting from implementation of the PHSM recommendations.
